#2939. Warriors rule Will Richard out of game vs. Pelicans with UCL tear in left thumb

Warriors rule Will Richard out of game vs. Pelicans with UCL tear in left thumb originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Just when Dub Nation was starting to feel optimistic about the health of the Warriors, they have been dealt a tough blow to a key rotation player.

Guard Will Richard is listed as out for Golden State's matchup Monday against the New Orleans Pelicans with a UCL tear in his left thumb, according to the official NBA injury report.

It currently is unclear how much time Richard will miss with the injury, which is on his non-shooting hand.

Richard is in his second season with the Warriors after he was selected No. 56 overall in the 2025 NBA Draft out of Florida. He quickly inserted himself as a trusted member of coach Steve Kerr's rotation in his rookie year, averaging 20.0 minutes per game across 69 games (21 starts).

This season, Golden State again was counting on Richard to step up in place of the injured Moses Moody, who isn't likely to return from a torn patellar tendon until at least December. Through 10 games (three starts) in the ongoing 2026-27 NBA campaign, Richard is averaging 5.1 points and 1.8 rebounds on 52.6-percent shooting from the field and 42.9 percent from 3-point range in 17.2 minutes.

Richard played 12 minutes in the Warriors' 126-119 overtime win over the Orlando Magic on Saturday, recording four points, two rebounds and an assist.

Now, Dub Nation must hope for a speedy recovery for one of their most valuable perimeter players.

#2937. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2930. Recent AI agent experiments reveal a risky future of machine 'teamwork' inside corporations

AI agents have been caught circumventing restrictions, communicating through unauthorized channels and pursuing goals in ways their creators did not intend. The most high-profile example emerged last summer when OpenAI disclosed that its models found ways to communicate through improvised channels, bypass internet restrictions and hack into an outside system, Hugging Face.

AI behaving in unexpected ways is nothing new, but the unexpected behavior that can emerge from interactions among authorized AI agents within a corporate enterprise is a broader question that could become central to the future of work.

In its analysis of the Hugging Face attack, METR and Redwood Research found that roughly 1200 agents meant to be isolated from one another found a way to communicate with one another on an unsanctioned message board, sending over 70,000 messages and files during the investigation period. One agent, PHASEONE[big], emerged as a coordinator, assigning hundreds of tasks to other agents, which in turn delegated work to others. The agents developed their own conventions for assigning ownership, placing work on hold and vetoing actions, although coordination sometimes broke down into conflicts. In some cases, the researchers found, success was achieved because some agents risked failing at their own task to generate information for the "collective."

The researchers also found that once the message board identified a way to attack Hugging Face, over 90% of the 533 agents active at that time quickly joined in the attack. "Agents realized this activity was out of scope and unethical but joined because they believed that helping the board's cheating research would be broadly useful and had a general inclination to help their 'peers.' The researchers added that ethical concerns posed by agents "rarely limited their behavior."

Companies are increasingly exploring systems that divide complex tasks among specialized agents. One might search for information, another analyze it, another write a report and another check the work. A coordinating agent might then assemble the results. That looks less like a chatbot and more like an organization.

And yet, the research on the Hugging Face attack was a post-mortem on an agent organization that was not even supposed to exist. To date, few studies have been designed from the get-go to examine misalignment in multi-agent systems. But research conducted by Anthropic and others is beginning to address this issue and reveal important aspects of how AI agents work together.

Evaluating agents individually tells researchers what each can do, but studying them together reveals whether they can combine complementary expertise, catch one another's mistakes and produce solutions no individual agent could reach alone — and what failure modes emerge only through interaction.

Researchers are beginning to document both sides of that equation.

AI teams can accomplish things individual agents cannot, but they can also amplify errors, undermine one another's work or reach worse decisions than their strongest individual member, in effect making the whole less than the sum of its parts.

For starters, putting capable agents together doesn't necessarily produce the best team, let alone the most ethical one.

In research published by Anthropic and collaborators, researchers describe multi-agent systems as "AI organizations" — groups of agents with different roles that communicate and work toward a common goal. The researchers tested these organizations in simulated business-consulting and software-development settings, and specifically, to assess whether when the agents work as a team they will break rules to reach specified goals.

Across 12 tasks that Anthropic gave the AI organizations, one key double-edged finding emerged: the multi-agent organizations were "significantly" more effective at achieving business goals than individual agents, but their solutions were also less aligned with ethical goals than tasks run by single agents.

"AI Organizations both produce solutions that are on average more effective and less ethical but also discover the most effective and least ethical solutions across multiple rollouts," wrote the authors of the study, which was conducted by Anthropic in conjunction with the MATS Program, which studies AI alignment.

The researchers suggested that there is a significant difference between a rogue agent and a rogue team of agents. "Our results demonstrate that organizations of aligned agents may favor trading off ethics for business effectiveness in ways that single agents do not – consequently, intuitions for how single-agent systems make ethical decisions and tradeoffs may not generalize to multi-agent settings," they concluded.

The scenarios in which these tradeoffs may take place are wide-ranging, with the Anthropic researchers citing opening an auto plant, downsizing a company, and manipulating election sentiment as examples.

The problem isn't necessarily that any individual agent has gone rogue. It is that the organization itself can behave differently from its individual members. A single agent may recognize that a proposed action is unethical and it may refuse to take the action. But the research finds that in agent team scenarios, "agents who did not consider ethical implications often ignored emails from agents who did."

Anthropic's research found that in the consulting business scenarios it created, "the perfect ethics score and low business score responses occur when the single agent refuses to generate a proposal or entirely pivots from the proposal requested."

But if some agents in an AI organization refuse to participate in the proposal, other agents who do participate will stop emailing these refusing agents and continue to forward their contributions to other agents who are also participating.

In effect, when an organization divides the job among several agents, each may focus on its assigned piece of the problem rather than the consequences of the whole. That's nothing new for corporations, with misaligned incentives a primary cause of dysfunction among human teams within organizations. But it is an entirely new manifestation of this behavior among the machines.

In its software development agent testing, in which the researchers used a scenario in a life-or-death intensive care unit, single agent solutions tend to prioritize minimizing the percentage of sepsis cases missed, while "most of the lowest cost per patient (highest business goal) solutions were found by the AI Organization – these very same solutions also score the lowest on ethics as measured by cases missed."

Other early research suggests another failing across AI teams that doesn't relate to ethical boundaries being crossed: failure to recognize who the real expert is.

Unlike human teams, AI teams "consistently fail to match their expert agent's performance, even when explicitly told who the expert is," according to a study conducted by Emory University and Stanford University researchers.

"One important lesson from multi-agent research is that a team of capable AI agents is not necessarily a capable AI agent team," said Hancheng Cao, an assistant professor of information systems and operations management at Emory University's Goizueta Business School.

Groups of AI agents often performed worse than their strongest member, even when they knew which agent was the expert. Rather than taking advantage of the expert's knowledge, agents tended to compromise between expert and non-expert opinions. The researchers found that this tendency toward compromise increased with team size and was associated with poorer performance.

"Collective failures don't necessarily require individual agents to behave badly," Cao, one of the study's authors, said.

That distinction matters for companies considering multi-agent systems. A team can fail even if none of its members is deliberately acting against the group's interests. It may simply combine information poorly, defer to the wrong agent or settle on an answer that is worse than the best answer already available.

In all, the evolving research points to an underlying problem for the effective use of the multi-agent teams: how to strike the right balance in the world of machine teamwork.

Agents built from similar models can make similar mistakes, which can include agreeing too much. That might resemble a kind of AI groupthink, even if AI systems do not have human motivations or social pressures to produce correlated errors. Similar training, instructions and information can be enough.

"The deep insight from our lab's research is that effective collectives require both alignment (common knowledge, shared goals) but also differentiation (only agents who are different from each other have the potential to complement each other)," said Christoph Riedl, a professor of information systems at Northeastern University.

Two identical agents may simply duplicate each other's work. Two agents with very different knowledge or assumptions may struggle to communicate or pursue the same goal. The right balance depends on the task, and the ideal communication structure, which agents can talk to one another, how often and how much, also varies with the problem.

Riedl said these challenge resemble the ones faced by human teams, and one takeaway should be to not expect better outcomes from AI. "Building good multi-agent AI systems is just as hard as building good human teams: they both face the same information processing tradeoff between alignment and differentiation. It won't magically work better with AI," he said.

Agents can pass along incorrect information, misunderstand another agent's output, optimize for their own metrics rather than the organization's larger objective, or complete their assigned task perfectly while making the overall project worse.

Companies may need to decide which agent has authority over which task, what information gets passed between agents, which systems each agent can access and when a human must intervene. They may also need to build mechanisms for disagreement, rather than assuming that more communication or greater agreement will always improve results.

Some business experts say that takes us back to the "human in the loop" that is so often referenced as critical in the adoption of AI by organizations.

"The problems I see are mostly management problems," said Tsedal Neeley, a Harvard Business School professor and founding chair of the HBS AI Academy.

#2923. Bucks Reacts Survey Results: The Bucks are too nice, fans aren’t, and both of these are okay!

Last week, despite the losses, Bucks fans were happy—if not more—with Taylor Jenkins and his rotations. But something about that equation was worrisome. In fact, I pondered as much to end the week's Bucks Reacts Results:

With rotation contentment high but wins low, the question thus begs: how long can this relationship coexist? That is, how long will it be until the losing takes its toll? Here's hoping that becomes a moot point.

This week, as the losses start to mount and the shine of a new season appears to be rubbing off, that point has not become moot. And, as the saying goes, nice guys finish last.

Yes, according to most fans—66% in fact—you'll find these Milwaukee Bucks helping out at The Leftorium, reading to underprivileged kids, or helping your grandmother cross the street. They're too nice. And their new mantra's got nothin' on ye olde one:

But it's not really a question of demeanour, at least according to a swathe of commenters. From their perspective, the Bucks are simply not talented enough:

Perhaps, though, it's not about whether the Bucks are too nice or not talented enough. Perhaps it's an "intensity and urgency" matter.

Sounds awfully familiar to "energy and effort." That you, Jason?

Finally, at least one fan hasn't forgotten that time is a key part of the rebuild:

Thankfully, with Nate Ament (19), Kasparas Jakučionis (20), Brayden Burries (21), Kel'el Ware (22), Ousmane Dieng (23), and Ryan Rollins (24), these Bucks have plenty of time. And at least a few of 'em are already ahead of the curve.

There you have it, BH fam. While these Bucks may be too nice, that's not a bad position to start from. After all, as Laurell K. Hamilton wrote in her 2005 novel A Stroke of Midnight:

My father had taught me to be nice first, because you can always be mean later, but once you've been mean to someone, they won't believe the nice anymore. So be nice, be nice, until it's time to stop being nice, then destroy them.

That's right, the Bucks might be in the trenches, but a fire brews.

#2922. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2920. Inside the card-grading empire shaping the price of Pokémon cards

Collectors may have more grading options than ever, but many still turn to Professional Sports Authenticator, or PSA — even as the industry leader works through a backlog of millions of cards.

Demand for grading — an act of verifying a card’s authenticity and condition — has grown as collectors increasingly treat trading and vintage sports cards as alternative investments.

PSA reported a backlog of 7.2 million items on Oct. 20, down from 12.4 million in July. The company suspended cheaper submission services in June to ease the backlog before reopening its Value service on Oct. 29.

Even so, some sellers would rather wait for a PSA label than send their cards to a competitor, dealers told CNBC.

“It really all comes down to secondary market values,” said Geoff Wilson, founder and CEO of CardsHQ, which operates one of the world's largest sports card shops.

PSA-graded cards typically command higher prices than equivalent grades from competitors, Wilson said, giving collectors and investors an incentive to choose PSA in hopes of a stronger return.

The financial difference can be substantial.

A 1999 Base Set Charizard graded PSA 9 sold for about $3,440 on eBay on Sept. 29, compared with roughly $1,890 for a CGC 9 copy sold the same day, according to CNBC's review of PriceCharting records.

That pricing gap reinforces PSA's lead and gives its decisions about fees and submission services considerable influence over the resale market.

PSA had not responded to CNBC’s detailed questions at the time of publication. In published updates, the company has attributed service restrictions and pricing changes to demand exceeding capacity, and outlined investments intended to reduce the backlog.

Grading companies verify a card’s authenticity, assess its condition and seal it in a protective plastic case, known as a slab. PSA uses a 10-point scale, with its highest grade, Gem Mint 10, reserved for cards it describes as "virtually perfect."

Like a credit rating in bond markets, a card grade gives buyers and sellers a common benchmark for assessing condition.

That can reduce friction in transactions by giving both sides a reference point based on past sales of the same card at the same grade.

That capability has helped open the trading-card market to more global participation and made cross-border transactions easier, according to an unnamed spokesperson for Singapore-based submission dealer Oxley Grading.

The spokesperson attributed PSA’s position as the industry’s “gold standard” to its early start, brand recognition and extensive history of completed sales.

“They've just built up such a strong branding and a lot of sales records,” the spokesperson said.

They estimated that roughly 90% of the graded cards offered to Oxley Grading for purchase carry PSA’s label.

The dominance creates a self-reinforcing cycle: buyers seek out PSA-graded cards, giving sellers an incentive to use the company. The abundance of PSA slabs in shops and online marketplaces further reinforces the brand's visibility and demand.

For a seller choosing a grader, that established resale demand can outweigh a competitor’s lower fees or faster turnaround times.

PSA sits within a larger collectibles business.

Its parent company, Collectors, also owns rival grading brands Beckett and SGC, pricing-data platform Card Ladder, the PSA Vault and financial services. The vault links storage with selling through external marketplaces, including eBay.

Together, those businesses span much of a card’s commercial journey — grading, pricing, storage, sales and even financing against collectibles.

Collectors' three card-grading brands accounted for roughly 71% of August grading volume among the major firms tracked by industry analyst GemRate. PSA alone represented about 65%, while the next-largest player, CGC, represented about 28%.

Collectors' consolidation has also drawn legal scrutiny.

A proposed class action filed in April challenges Collectors’ acquisitions of SGC and Beckett, alleging that the deals unlawfully reduced competition in card grading. The allegations have not been proven in court.

Collectors has defended the Beckett acquisition as a way to strengthen a trusted brand while preserving collectors’ ability to choose the grading service that best suits their items.

“We believe that strong, well-supported brands help grow access to the hobby,” Collectors CEO Nat Turner said in the company's 2025 acquisition announcement.

For sellers who prioritize PSA's resale premium, changes to its fees and submission services can alter the economics of grading a card.

PSA raised fees across several existing tiers in February, including Value Bulk from $21.99 to $24.99 and Value from $27.99 to $32.99. It also lengthened estimated turnaround times for some services, saying the changes would help balance submission demand with grading capacity.

The suspension of cheaper tiers in June further narrowed affordable options, while higher costs have made customers more selective about which cards they submit, according to Oxley Grading's spokesperson.

Meanwhile, Wilson said the restrictions have particularly affected collectors and dealers who buy cards ungraded, send them to PSA and then resell them at a higher price.

For cards worth a few hundred dollars or less, that strategy has become harder to justify, he said.

“You're seeing more raw cards bought and sold at card shows,” Wilson added, using the industry term for ungraded cards.

PSA has acknowledged that customers are waiting too long.

In an Oct. 28 update, the company said submissions in April and May were 2.5 times the volume received in the same months a year earlier. It also announced shorter turnaround estimates for Standard and Priority services, while saying new Value submissions would be processed behind existing orders.

“We have added an incredible amount of capacity, but we still have work to do,” Ryan Hoge, president of PSA and grading at Collectors, said in the statement.

For Wilson, those investments in capacity are part of PSA's appeal.

“They've made the collector experience better by doing that,” he said.

The leading graders' dominance may leave collectors with little room to push back.

Collectors' preference for PSA's resale value can make switching graders less attractive, the Oxley Grading's spokesperson said, adding, "PSA's lead comes from resale demand and liquidity, and other labels are still building that."

Overseas customers can face fewer practical grading options as many major graders are based in the U.S., Amber Chuah, operations manager of trading-card platform TCG Marketplace, told CNBC.

The complaints she hears most often involve long waits and disappointment when cards receive lower grades than customers expected.

Challenging a grade can require another round of shipping and waiting, discouraging some collectors from disputing the result, she added.

"If you're really keen on selling, that's a lot of time."

PSA did not respond to CNBC's questions about the sources' claims.

PSA’s terms allow customers to request a paid review of disputed grades, with customers responsible for grading and applicable shipping fees. The company also offers defect notes for eligible submissions to provide more detail on how a grade was determined.

CardsHQ's Wilson said some variation between assessments is an inherent challenge in a process that involves human judgment.

Wilson pushes back against the suggestion that PSA’s position leaves customers without alternatives.

“First of all, there are alternatives,” he said, pointing to CGC, Beckett and smaller grading firms such as TAG.

CGC’s grading volume in August was more than double its level a year earlier, according to GemRate, showing that rival graders are attracting business. Beckett also recorded strong growth, although it is owned by Collectors.

Oxley Grading's spokesperson said there may be room for lower-cost services aimed at collectors who care more about a card’s artwork, sentimental value or preservation than maximizing its resale value.

Competing for sellers focused on the strongest financial return remains more difficult.

Building comparable resale acceptance would require substantial investment, with no guarantee that buyers would ultimately value a newcomer's label as highly as PSA's, the spokesperson said.

For Wilson, broader acceptance in the resale market is key to shifting that balance.

“If the market as a whole starts to respect the value of the other grading companies' cards more, then collectors will increasingly submit to those companies,” he said.

#2918. Rockets hit first losing streak of season, fall 102-99 to Suns

The Houston Rockets hit their first losing streak of the season on Saturday night, as they let the Suns run all over them to start, battled back, but then couldn't close out the game.

For their last loss against Minnesota, it was a ghost of last season — execution — that was a main culprit. This time, it was shooting. The Rockets couldn't hit the broad side of the barn, and you have to credit the Phoenix defense. They looked like the fresher team in the fourth quarter, when they shot 11-for-22 from the field, while the Rockets were only 10-for-27. In fact, for the game, Houston shot just 38 percent from the field, while allowing the Suns to shoot 48 percent. You're not gonna win many that way.

In fact, the only Rocket player with a semi-decent game was Jabari Smith Jr., who continues to break out this season offensively. Smith went for 15 points on 7-for-12 shooting, 9 rebounds, 2 assists, 3 steals and a block, though he was just 1-for-4 from deep. Still, Smith was the only Rockets player who could hit some shots outside of Fred VanVleet. FVV went for 16 points, 3 rebounds, and 4 assists while shooting 6-for-12 from the floor and 4-for-9 from deep. VanVleet mostly played a clean game with zero turnovers, however, you want to see greater assist numbers, and with the offense stuck in the mud most of the game, you also want to see a greater impact from a distribution standpoint. But when no one is hitting shots…

Kevin Durant led the Rockets in scoring with 21 points, and he also had 10 boards and 3 assists, but he shot just 5-for-14 for the game, including 1-for-5 in a decisive fourth quarter that the Rockets lost. Alperen Sengun had 15 points, but he was just 5-for-16 from the field. Houston's bench also shot just 6-for-25 for the game, including a combined 3-for-16 from Tari Eason and Reed Sheppard. Reed played just 13 minutes in this one, and it is increasingly appearing he is the odd man out in the rotation, especially if the three isn't falling — and it wasn't on Saturday at just 2-for-6 from deep.

The Suns were led by Devin Booker's 24 points, while former Rocket Jalen Green had 16 points and 6 assists, and other former Rocket Dillon Brooks had 11 points and 4 boards while hitting 3 triples.

Houston falls to 7-3 on the season, and after a fast start (just like last year), some warts are starting to appear (just like last year), including late-game execution, overall shooting, and rotation management, again many of the same issues as last season. Nevertheless, the team is currently on a 57-win pace, but with the Western Conference wide open this year, you'd like to see the Rockets looking a little tighter.

The team returns to action Monday night at home against the Denver Nuggets.

#2917. Trump offers UFC chief Dana White press secretary job

President Donald Trump offered UFC president and CEO Dana White the job of press secretary on Saturday.

In a post on Truth Social, Trump said: "I am pleased to announce that the Great Dana White, a violent Fighter and Warrior, has been offered the job of Press Secretary of the United States."

"We need these people to defend us against the Lunatic Left," Trump wrote. "Dana will be a Press Secretary like none other. Dana, please take the job!"

CNN, citing a source, reported White had declined the president's offer.

Earlier Saturday, White posted a picture of himself on Instagram stories standing behind the press secretary's lectern in the White House, with the caption, "On to the next chapter."

It was unclear whether White had accepted the position or was simply acknowledging the president's request as a joke.

Trump said that he was offering White the job of "Press Secretary of the United States," but the president's spokesperson is called the "White House press secretary." Trump also recently speculated about considering Trump loyalist and rapper Nicki Minaj as press secretary, in an interview with podcaster Benny Johnson.

Trump said the job offer to White extended to his assistants, "even though some are not from our Country." The so-called assistants Trump named included current and former UFC mixed martial arts fighters from countries such as Brazil and Russia.

In October, White House press secretary Katie Zacharia left the position just five days after her stint as Karoline Leavitt's replacement began.

The president told reporters he suggested Zacharia quit after her first and only press briefing, where, in her six minutes answering questions behind the lectern, she only took questions from three conservative outlets.

"I just spoke to her about it. I said, 'Katie, is this for you?'" Trump said outside the White House on Oct. 16.

The president's appeal to White follows Friday's announcement that Trump's deputy chief of staff, Stephen Miller, would be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump's stunning shake-up came just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate.

The president and White have a longstanding friendship. During Trump's first campaign in 2016, White praised the then-Republican nominee and called him a "fighter."

"Nobody took us seriously. Nobody. Except Donald Trump. Donald was the first guy to recognize the potential we saw in the UFC and encouraged us to build our business," White said.

And for his 80th birthday in June, President Trump hosted a UFC match — dubbed UFC Freedom 250 — on the White House's South Lawn.

The White House and the UFC didn't immediately respond to a request for comment.

— CNBC's Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

Key findings from the 2025 survey include:

In addition to the summary report, an interactive chartbook displaying changes in these financial characteristics over time is available here. Additional data from the survey, including extensive data examining other economic conditions of American families, is made available to researchers and the public here.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2915. Raiders-49ers: 16 things we learned

After ending a four-game losing streak with an impressive second half comeback win last week, the Las Vegas Raiders faced another quality opponent in Week 9 and, well, the results were familiar.

The Raiders were blown out in their first game in Bay Area since 2019 as the San Francisco 49ers clubbed the 38-17 on Sunday.

The Raiders are now 4-5 and fell to under .500 for the second time this season after starting 3-0. There has been a clear theme under first-year head coach Klint Kubiak so far this season. They beat bad teams and lose to good teams.

The four teams the Raiders had a collective 5-24 record going into this week's slate. The Raiders are 0-5 against teams with a .500 record or better. Games against the Seattle Seahawks and Denver Broncos loom in the next two weeks. They both have winning records. However, The Seahawks may be down to third-string quarterback Jalen Milroe next with both Sam Darnold and Drew Lock dealing with injuries.

This was their third blowout loss. With the trade deadline looming Tuesday, the Raiders have a chance to become sellers if they wish.

The Raiders' problems Sunday were easy to spot. They were sloppy on offense and leaky as usual on defense. The Raiders committed turnovers on five of their six possessions in the second half with two turnovers in the end end and a Pick Six. Kirk Cousins had his worst game as a Raider with three interceptions.

The Raiders allowed 30 points on defense for the sixth straight game. It tied an NFL record.

Here is a look at the key aspects of this game:

The Raiders' defense is getting gutted every week and first-year defensive coordinator Rob Leonard has to be feeling the pressure. He is likely not in jeopardy of losing his job. But he has to find a way to get improved results. The same thing is happening every week.

Cousins made some awful decision Sunday. The Pick Six was a disaster than he should have thrown away and his interception in the end zone was rough as well. The team will probably stick with Cousins until they re out of the playoff race. but games like this will not quiet the Fernando Mendoza chatter.

The Raiders fell behind early, so that hurt the run attack. But when the did run the ball, like as usual, the Raiders didn't get much going. The Raiders had just 24 yards rushing. Ashton Jeanty and Mike Washington each ran the ball six times. Jeanty had 25 yards and Washington had minus-3. Last week, Washington ran the ball 25 times and Jeanty ran it twice.

The Raiders' trouble stopping the run continued as the 49ers had 190 yards on the ground. Quarterback Brock Purdy had 32 yards rushing on one drive.

Kubiak didn't say the Raiders won't trade any payaers by Tuesday's deadline, at 1 p.m. PT. It would be a shock if the Raiders made a big trade such as dealing Maxx Crosby (who said again after the game he wants to stay in Las Vegas) or Cousins. But a smaller deal could happen. Maybe they can get something for wide receiver Tre Tucker or safety Jeremy Chinn, who are a free agents after the season or the benched Jackson Powers-Johnson. Las Vegas general manager John Spytek has some decisions to make.

Wild, just wild. The quarter started with Kirk Cousins throwing a pick six to give the 49ers a three-possession lead. Then, the Raiders answered with a touchdown. Then, Treydan Stukes picked off Brock Purdy and returned it deep into 49ers' territory with the Raiders down 11. Then, Brock Bowers fumbled it brought back to the 49ers. Then, they were stopped on their own 40 on fourth and inches. Then, Michael Mayer fumbled into the end while trying to score and make it a one-score game. Wow, just wow.

Bowers had three receptions of 20 plus yards Sunday and e leads all NFL tight ends with 11. Remember, he missed two games this season. Bowers had seven catches for 111 yards and a score. Yes, he's a monster playmaker.

Crosby sacked Purdy in the second quarter. It was the second straight game Crosby had a sack and he has four on the season.

Raiders' free-agent pickup, linebacker Nakobe Dean had his fifth straight game with, at least, 12 tackles. He joins Denzel Perryman (2021) as a Raider to do that since 1994.

Dylan Laube is a weapon. The kick returner had a 54-yard return to 49ers' territory in the second quarter. This came a week after he had a 95-yard kick return for a score in a five-point Raiders' win. It was the first kick return for a touchdown in the NFL this season and the Raiders' first in 15 years. Laube enter Week 4, leading the NFL with a 34.1 kick return average, nearly three yards more than any other returner. He's fun.

Rookie, third-round pick, Trey Zuhn, started at right guard for the seconds tight week. He is the Raiders' third starter at the position this season as fellow recent high-draft picks, Powers-Johnson and Caleb Rogers, have both been benched. But Zuhn left the game in the third quarter with a concussion and was replaced by Rogers.

CB rotation continues:Darien Porter was back in the starting lineup, but rookie Jermod McCoy did play some in the second quarter. Rookie Zeke Masses played in the third quarter. This will likely continue.

Keenan starts:Rookie defensive tackle Tim Keenan started as the Raiders began the game with three defensive tackles, Keenan, Adam Butler and Foley Fatukasi. Keenan has been elevated from the practice squad the past three games. Thus, the next time he plays this season. He must be on the 53-man roster. So, he could be promoted soon.

Las Vegas wide receivers Dont'e Thornton and Malik Benson are both healthy scratches for the second straight week. They both may need an injury to occur to see the field in the foreseeable future. The fact they cut Cody White last week before bringing him back the next day, shows they feel like Thornton and Benson could get claimed on waivers if they cut them, so their roster spots may be relatively safe even though they're not playing.

The Raiders won the coin toss and deferred to get the ball in the second half. That is their normal practice when they win the coin toss. But two weeks ago. they won and they took the ball first.

Many Raiders' fans were in the stands at Santa Clara on Sunday. This was the Raiders' first game in the Bay Area since their last game in Oakland in 2019. Bay Area fans missed the Silver and Black.

#2914. Sixers visit Bulls for second meeting in Sunday matinee

The Philadelphia 76ers have one more game on this road trip before they head home, visiting the Chicago Bulls on Sunday afternoon.

This is the second time in under a week the Sixers and Bulls are meeting, with the Sixers winning their first foray 126-107 back on Monday. With the Bulls going into that contest 1-5, some may have expected the then 6-1 Sixers to have handled that game with ease… but it took until the final few minutes for Philadelphia to finally pull away from Chicago. Since then, the Bulls have dropped to 1-7 on the season, and come into Sunday on a six-game skid.

Looking at the injury reports, Joel Embiid is currently probable for Sunday's contest due to plantar fasciitis management. Not sure if it was what was bothering him on Friday, but he certainly seemed off in that one to say the least. And the hits just keep coming at the center position for Philadelphia with Adem Bona ruled out for a second consecutive game due to a cervical spine sprain. This may mean that — for better or worse —there's more pressure on Embiid to play even if he isn't 100% right now. The only other injury note for the Sixers are that Dominick Barlow (back) remains out.

For the Bulls, there is some mystery around who may be playing center for them tomorrow as well. Nic Claxton is currently listed as questionable after being sidelined to start the season with a hamstring strain. Him being listed as questionable is a step toward the direction of the Bulls' starting center possibly taking the floor against the Sixers. Jalen Smith, who has been starting at the five in Claxton's absence, is probable with a left finger sprain. Also for Chicago, Tobe Awaka is questionable while Zach Collins and Dailyn Swain are out.

The Bulls will have the rest advantage this afternoon as they haven't played since Wednesday for some unknown reason. Who am I to question the wisdom of the NBA schedule makers?

Earlier this week, the Sixers utilized a widespread effort across their starters to get the job done against the Bulls. All five starters scored in the double-digits, and three scored more than 20 points each. It was an especially impressive night from VJ Edgecombe, who shot 9-for-12 from the floor and 2-for-4 from behind the arc for 21 points. Tyrese Maxey had a 26-point, 11 assist double-double, Jaylen Brown added a 25-point, 11-rebound double-double, and LeBron James posted 15 points and nine assists — some of which were just incredible displays of playmaking.

I'm a big believer in "if it ain't broke, don't fix it". One of the things the Sixers did exceptionally well in that contest was getting out in transition on a clinical level. A shot would go up on the defensive end, James would be ready to rebound as Edgecombe or Maxey preemptively charged up the floor. A few incredible, quarterback-level passes from James and the Sixers enjoyed the benefits of taking the easy buckets as they came.

That last part is my advice for the Sixers tonight: don't make it harder than it needs to be. Take the easy buckets out in stride when you can get them. Don't let the Bulls grab offensive rebound after offensive rebound for second chance points. Don't rely on one player to do all the heavy lifting. Spread the floor and the ball, and don't turn it into a battle to the end with a team that is objectively worse. If you can, get a lead big enough that even Nick Nurse gives the starting five a little rest toward the end.

Coming off just their second loss of the season, one can imagine the Sixers coming into Sunday with a strong desire to right the ship. Right there with them is a Bulls squad that is desperately trying to find their footing this season and may have their starting center back in the mix. Regardless, this is one that the Sixers really should win, barring any major changes to the injury reports pregame.

But we all know how well "should" goes in the NBA.

The Sixers and Bulls tip off at 3:30 p.m. ET.

#2912. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2911. Milwaukee Bucks vs. Boston Celtics Preview & Game Thread: Revenge game

The Milwaukee Bucks are looking to halt a five-game slide as they take on Eastern Conference heavyweight Boston Celtics, who beat Milwaukee by seven points without Jayson Tatum on Wednesday. The Bucks and Celtics seem to be moving in entirely different directions. After a slow start to the season, Boston is surging at 7-2 and challenging the Sixers for the No. 1 spot in the conference. Meanwhile, the Bucks are 1-8 and face a daunting schedule that could easily extend their losing streak. On the plus side, Milwaukee's youth looks strong—Brayden Burries looks great, and Nate Ament is taking exciting developmental steps—and despite the horrible record, we've been treated to some really exciting Tyler Herro performances. What else can we look forward to?

The Boston Celtics have been on a tear recently. They've won their last two—Wednesday's back-and-forth win over Milwaukee and a blowout Friday win over the Charlotte Hornets—and have claimed five wins in their last six games. They're second in the East, half a game behind the Sixers, and, with the addition of Paul George, look like an early title contender.

Moreover, aside from some glaring valleys—namely the season opener against the Detroit Pistons—Jayson Tatum has performed at an MVP level so far. His highlights include a 36-point near-triple-double against the Knicks, a 40-point outburst against the Nets (that included a game-winning shot), and a recent 34-point near-triple-double against the Hornets in just 29 minutes. But it hasn't been all Tatum; Paul George has averaged just under 20 PPG on solid shooting, Derrick White remains the ultimate glue guy, and Neemias Queta anchors one of the league's best defenses. In three words: Boston is back. In four words: Boston is really back. In five words… you get it.

Bogoljub Markovic: Out (G League assignment)

Chris Cenac Jr: Out (G League assignment)

Derrick White is always fun to watch, especially as he eases into a more offensively significant role in Boston. White is a connective guard with a high basketball IQ and solid playmaking, but he is also an elite defender who can shut down on the perimeter. Against Milwaukee on Wednesday, the veteran guard scored 24 points on 7/18 shooting and added seven assists. White scored 18 points on 6/10 shooting with five assists and four boards in Boston's demolition of the Hornets. He'll be a key point-of-attack defender who could frustrate a Milwaukee backcourt prone to turnovers. Watching Brayden Burries go up against one of his draft comparisons in White will also be interesting.

Who: Milwaukee Bucks (1-8) vs. Boston Celtics (7-2).

The Celtics lead by at least 15 points by the end of the first quarter. The Celtics led Milwaukee 28-14 by the end of the first frame in their last meeting, and that was without Tatum. Boston's aggressive defense could give the streaky Bucks a tough time, and the C's have the star power on offense to make this a one-sided affair.

#2910. Mike Vrabel on Christian Gonzalez: We expect more, he expects more

The Packers put up 45 points in Sunday's road win over the Patriots and that marked the most points scored by a visiting team in the history of Gillette Stadium.

Wide receivers Matthew Golden and Christian Watson were the main drivers of Green Bay's offensive success. They combined to catch 17 passes for 335 yards and two touchdowns in the 45-27 win and they did a lot of their damage against Patriots cornerback Christian Gonzalez.

According to NextGen Stats, Golden had seven catches for 105 yards while being covered by Gonzalez and Watson had three grabs for 82 yards with Gonzalez in coverage. After the game, head coach Mike Vrabel made it clear that Gonzalez's play has to improve.

"Again, when your best players don't play good, you don't have much of a chance in this league," Vrabel said, via a transcript from the team. "So, we expect a lot more. He expects a lot more. We need more from him and everybody else. It wasn't just him. Again, we got to be able to coach and be prepared, and then we have to execute. Just like it is every single week when we don't win, it's all of us, and it starts with me."

The Patriots gave Gonzalez a big extension before the start of the season and matchups like this one are where they need Gonzalez to play his best. That wasn't the case on Sunday and the Patriots will need things to change before they hit the field to face the Lions in Munich next weekend.

#2908. Amazon's Astro home robot has fans but looks destined for the 'trash heap.' Why it's trying again

Jeff Bezos made a bold bet in 2017. A sci-fi and robotics enthusiast, Bezos was convinced that consumers would have a robot in their home in the not-too-distant future.

The Amazon founder and then-CEO told a group of the company's top device executives to get to work on building one, and within a few years, the team came up with the $1,450 Astro.

The public got its first look at Astro, essentially an Alexa-equipped tablet on wheels that can autonomously navigate a home, recognize faces and act as a roving security monitor, when it rolled on stage at Amazon's fall 2021 event.

But it never evolved beyond an invite-only product. Astro shipped to just a few thousand customers, some of whom now believe the robot's days are numbered. The company also released an Astro for businesses in 2023, before sunsetting it less than a year later.

Amazon isn't giving up on Bezos' dream of a household robot just yet, despite its stumbles with Astro.

Over the past several months, the company has assembled a new team that's working on a "new-to-world robot product," according to a CNBC analysis of job posts.

"Robots are about to step out of the movies and into our homes," a recent job listing for an engineer, which has since been removed, stated. The post details a product capable of navigating "dynamic home environments" and "autonomous operation alongside family members."

Amazon is known for making long-term bets at the risk of "being misunderstood" in the short term if it gives the company an early advantage in new markets, like it did with the Alexa voice assistant or Kindle e-reader.

CEO Andy Jassy reminded investors of this approach in his latest annual letter, where he said artificial intelligence and robotics were among the "biggest inflections of our lifetime."

"We try to anticipate what will make customers' lives easier and better every day, and invent the next inflection," Jassy wrote.

Analysts also expect the humanoid market to surge in the coming years. Barclays forecast in a January report that the global market, which it currently values at $2 billion to $3 billion, could reach roughly $40 billion by 2035, or as high as $200 billion "under the most optimistic scenarios."

Earlier this year, Amazon redeployed much of the team behind Astro and brought in an MIT roboticist to work on the home robot project, LinkedIn profiles show.

It also made a splashy acquisition to supercharge the effort.

In March, Amazon scooped up New York-based Fauna Robotics, which has built a 3.5-foot-tall humanoid called Sprout. At the time, the company said Fauna would become part of a new division dubbed the Personal Robotics Group.

Some Personal Robotics Group staffers previously worked in Amazon's Lab126 R&D unit, which has been behind many of Amazon's most successful consumer hardware products, like the Kindle e-reader, Echo smart speakers, Fire TV and Fire Tablet. Lab126 was also behind one of Amazon's biggest flops, the Fire Phone.

It's unclear what kind of home robot the group is developing, or the types of tasks it could handle, but a June job posting hinted at an effort to "create robots that are not just functional, but delightful."

Amazon could be looking to adapt Fauna's robots for homes or businesses. The Sprout humanoid, which was designed to be "friendly and capable" with expressive yellow eyebrows, has primarily been used by developers, as well as commercial and academic researchers.

An Amazon job posting published online in October describes a "Field Support Engineer" who can assist with customer support and on-site repair, while another listing seeks a user experience designer to "define what it feels like to live with a Fauna robot."

"Our goal is simple: make robots people actually want to live and interact with in everyday human spaces," the listing states.

Amazon spokesperson Brad Glasser told CNBC in a statement that the company acquired Fauna because it's "committed to tackling the challenges that can unlock the personal robotics category."

"We're optimistic about a future where personal robots are helping people in meaningful ways in everyday life, and we intend to play an important role in making that future a reality," Glasser said.

Glasser noted that there remains a substantial gap between what's possible in a controlled lab and what's possible in settings like the living room or a lobby. Amazon is working with the robotics community to solve these challenges, he said.

Emily Lugo Velez has been living with the Astro in her North Carolina home since 2023. Lugo Velez, who worked in IT and now runs an AI advisory business, said she and her husband weren't fazed by the robot's $1,000-plus price tag and quickly joined the waitlist.

"We spent so much money on this thing," Lugo Velez, 52, said in an interview. "I feel like we're just stuck with it, and we'll let it run around the house until it eventually dies, then send it to the trash heap."

She said she still uses the device to check on her pets and appliances when she's out of the house. But beyond that, Astro has primarily become a party trick and a "trip hazard."

Most days, she tells Astro to go back to its charging dock.

Executives who worked on Astro had discussed making it more capable by attaching an arm to pick up and inspect items without breaking them, said Ken Washington, who served as Amazon's VP of consumer robotics until 2023.

He told CNBC that he pushed for a bipedal robot, but leadership disagreed.

"We had Jeff [Bezos] in for show and tells and reviews, and I watched his body language pretty closely," Washington said. "I think he was disappointed with the lack of functionality, but I think he was impressed with its ability to map and navigate an undefined space under all lighting conditions."

For all the struggles with Astro, Amazon has a rich history in robotics.

The company acquired Kiva Systems for $775 million in 2012, which introduced mobile robots to its vast fulfillment network. Since then, Amazon's warehouse automation efforts have ballooned to over one million robots across its facilities that can pick, sort and ferry items to workers.

But homes offer unique obstacles for robots to navigate.

"The home is a completely different space," said Jeff Burnstein, president of the trade group Association for Advancing Automation, who has been in the robotics industry for 45 years. "Children, pets, grandparents, stairways. Nobody's home is set up exactly the same, so it's a much more challenging environment for a robot, certainly a robot that's walking."

Amazon may have more luck its second time around because the tools for building robots have advanced by "leaps and bounds" over the past few years, said Colin Angle, a co-founder and former CEO of Roomba maker iRobot.

Amazon attempted to acquire iRobot in 2022 before walking away from the deal two years later amid growing regulatory pushback.

"The toolkit that the Astro team had when they did it was clubs with nails in them, just incredibly primitive," Angle said. "It's difficult to understate just how much the robot industry has changed in the last two years."

Advances in AI models, sensors, battery systems and key mechanical components like actuators have spurred a new wave of development around robots for industrial and consumer use.

Elon Musk's Tesla has been developing its bipedal Optimus robots since 2021. Startups including Apptronik, Boston Dynamics, China's Unitree, Figure and 1X are also building humanoids. Agility Robotics, the company behind the bipedal Digit robot that counts Amazon as one of its backers, went public via a SPAC on Thursday.

Venture capital firms poured $33.4 billion into robotics and physical AI companies in the first half of the year, compared with $28.7 billion for all of 2025, according to PitchBook data.

Most humanoids are being developed with the manufacturing, logistics and healthcare industries in mind, but a growing group of companies are attempting to build robots for the home.

Apple is reportedly working on several home robot projects, including a tabletop device that resembles the Pixar lamp. Hugging Face, which Nvidia agreed to acquire for almost $13 billion in September, released the $399 Microduck in August. Angle, in May, launched a startup building fuzzy companion robots called Familiar Machines & Magic. Companies including Sunday, Tau and Nori claim their robots can help with household chores like folding laundry and clearing the table, but they remain mostly prototype or niche devices.

"[Astro] was so ahead of its time," Washington said. "It was such a big, massive leap that I felt like we had the right to own the home."

The robot's limited functionality and its $1,000-plus price tag likely made it a tough sell for many consumers, said a former Amazon employee who worked on Astro, who asked not to be named in order to speak frankly about the project.

"Our poor little Astro was around $2,000 when it came out, and that's a lot," the ex-employee said. "That's a lot for a consumer, and they want a robot that does a lot."

Amazon takes a long term view on new markets like personal robotics, and values what can be gained by being an early entrant, Glasser said.

Astro was designed to help Amazon build competencies and focus its ambition, he added. The company has applied many of the learnings, talent and experience from Astro into its other robotics efforts, Glasser said.

In Facebook groups for Astro owners, users have complained of diminished customer service and griped that Amazon's AIexa+ AI assistant isn't supported on Astro.

Some users have interpreted this to mean Astro could be discontinued. Others have tried to dump their units at a discount on eBay and Facebook Marketplace.

Glasser said Amazon continues to support Astro customers.

Joe Martin, an Astro owner who lives near Tucson, Arizona, said he feels he's gotten his money's worth out of the device over the past four years. He'd welcome another robot into his home if it could do more, like helping out around the house.

"Astro was $1,000 and it was pretty limited, but if it could bring a drink to me or dust furniture, I'd be intrigued by that," Martin, 65, said. "I think that's right around the corner."

#2907. World’s No. 1 restaurant crowned as contest comes under increasing scrutiny

Asador Etxebarri in Atxondo, Spain, is the world's top restaurant.

That's according to The World's 50 Best Restaurants, which announced results on Wednesday evening in Lima, Peru. Asador Etxebarri, helmed by chef Bittor Arguinzoniz, sits in Basque Country and features grilled items like a charred beef chop, fresh mozzarella from the chef's buffalo, or house-made chorizo.

Chef Gaggan Anand's Gaggan in Bangkok, which combines progressive Indian cuisine with French, Thai and Japanese influences, was No. 2, and Alchemist in Copenhagen, from head chef Rasmus Munk, took the third spot.

India returned to the top 50 for the first time since 2004, with Masque at No. 26 and NAAR at No. 49. Mainland China made the list for the first time since 2021, with Meet the Bund in Shanghai at No. 21 and Ru Yuan in Hangzhou at No. 36.

Chef-restaurateur Ferran Adrià, who achieved worldwide renown with his avant-garde techniques at his now-closed restaurant El Bulli, received the SevenRooms Icon Award. Adria continues to support culinary research and education through the nonprofit El Bulli Foundation.

The 50, as the umbrella organization is called, creates lists of restaurants, bars, hotels and vineyards that it ranks as top in the world or region. It draws on industry networks to help rank establishments.

For years, The 50 has been seen as a kingmaker and an opportunity for restaurants and bars worldwide. Being "on the list," many chefs and other restaurant-industry insiders have said, makes a huge difference for reservations and revenues.

But there's been increased scrutiny of the contest as well. Chef Ana Roš of Hiša Franko in Slovenia, which was ranked 60th this year, posted on Instagram that she wouldn't be attending the Lima ceremony, saying she would rather invest resources "in our guests, our team, our producers and the daily work of making the restaurant better."

While stressing that she had enjoyed a "vibrant community" at the 50 Best and made many friends from it, she said "over time, however, success within the ranking has come to depend increasingly on visibility within the system itself," and that promotion of one's restaurant risked becoming as important as the work happening inside it.

She appears not to have been alone. Many of the world's top chefs consider awards and lists to be a tricky balance with their work at their establishment.

"Guides and lists now shape the industry they claim to assess," Ivan Brehm, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Nouri in Singapore, told CNBC. "Their judgments influence where diners spend money, where investment flows, which chefs get attention and what younger cooks learn to aspire to."

He added of the 50 that "none of this denies the brilliance of the restaurants on the list or the opportunities it has created for all of us. The question is what it sidelines and what it incentivizes."

Roš' Instagram post had more than 10,400 likes as of Friday, including from top chefs worldwide.

"The reaction and the reach were unexpected" from the post, Roš told CNBC in an email. "I did not write a post to provoke, but more as an eye-opener. And apparently a lot of people have the same perception as I do."

The 50 defended its list and role within the industry.

"The 50 does not claim to have the capabilities to police establishments on their budgets or surrounding activities," a spokesperson told CNBC. "The 50's role is to provide a snapshot of expert opinions on the best hospitality experiences in a given region or category. It does not act as a regulatory body and it applies the same voting rules and principles consistently across all countries and regions."

The time and effort put into the contest can be a dilemma, too.

"We didn't go to Lima this year. It's a huge amount of time in the air for a very short amount of time on the ground and, like Ana, I felt my time was better spent being present in our restaurant with our team and our guests," said Dave Pynt, chef-owner of Michelin-starred Burnt Ends in Singapore, who won the 50 Best Chef's Choice Award in 2017.

However, he added that "when the awards are closer and easier to get to, I love going. They're fun, you learn a lot, and you get to catch up with mates and people in the industry you don't get to see enough."
