#2905. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2903. Federal Reserve Board announces approval of application by The Bank of Nova Scotia

#2902. Knicks Week Preview: Nov. 8-14, 2026

The Knicks have bobbed over .500 for the first time this season, thanks to yesterday's return to form against the Cleveland Cavaliers. Over the upcoming week, our heroes will get a second whack at the Ohio Players, plus two beatable opponents. Let's take a peek at the schedule, shall we?

The Bucks are in a funky funk. Sitting last in the east, they pull into town having lost five straight and seven of their eight games this season. This has not been an auspicious start for new coach Taylor Jenkins. Over the summer, they lost Giannis Antetokounmpo and got younger, which has resulted in struggles at both ends of the floor. Tuesday's game will be their first meeting with the Knicks this season. The last time they tussled, back on February 27, was a 127-98 New York win in Milwaukee.

Our heroes should have many chances to run against Milwaukee and plenty of mismatches to pick on. The Bucks don't necessarily lack talent—Tyler Herro (acquired in the Giannis trade) leads the team at 22.3 points per game—just hope.

The Cavs just got a good look at what New York does well, and boy did it make their eyes water. Four nights after beating the Cavs 109-97 in Cleveland, the two rivals square up again at the Garden. Brunson scored 31 in the first tilt, Karl-Anthony Towns added 29 points and 13 rebounds, and New York led by 19 in the third. Donovan Mitchell found his way to 37, fighting to the end; James Harden died of old age; and Evan Mobley melted into a sweaty puddle under the rim.

Cleveland might have been fatigued on Saturday (playing a SEGABABA), and coach Kenny Atkinson will surely try to mix things up in their rematch. We're curious to see what adjustments are up his sleeve. Jarrett Allen might return from the injured list, so maybe he can keep up with KAT better than Mopey Mobs?

The Knicks finish the week in Indiana. The poor Pacers can make a mess of things if you give them the ball and a head start. The 3-7 club has had trouble getting stops and closing games, but you can chalk that up to their leader Tyrese Haliburton being injured again. If he returns for Friday's game, this could be a different contest.

The Pacers can still score in a hurry, especially if you help them with turnovers and lost rebounds. Without Haliburton, Pascal Siakam and Andrew Nembhard have carried the load. Rick Carlisle's club can turn into layups at the other end, and for as inept as they've been at times this season, they can still hit the gas. According to my notes, you'll watch this one on Prime Video.

#2900. 31-year-old dumpster diver brought in $140,000 in 12 months: I 'did much more than I thought was possible'

When Carter Stevens tells people he makes six figures a year digging through dumpsters, they often assume he's "blowing smoke," he says.

Not everyone sees dollar signs in a brimming dumpster. But Stevens earned roughly $140,000 in total sales between September 2025 and September 2026 by salvaging trashed items, cleaning them up and selling them on platforms like eBay and Facebook Marketplace, according to documents reviewed by CNBC Make It.

Stevens primarily operates near office parks and luxury condominiums around Nashville, Tennessee. Since March 2024, when he started dumpster diving, he's found and sold hand-crafted Persian rugs, high-end office chairs and even samurai swords, he says. Trash-picking helps him keep his shipping costs low, he adds: Some of his supplies, like cardboard boxes and packing tape, also come from dumpsters, enough that he doesn't have to pay for them approximately "90% of the time."

"The amount of waste in this country that you'll find is unbelievable. We throw so much stuff out," says Stevens, 31.

Sometimes, Stevens asks a building for permission to go through its trash, marketing himself as a junk removal service. In that way, his job is similar — though not identical, because he generally only takes what he can sell, he says — to other entrepreneurs across the U.S. who follow advice in Reddit threads or YouTube tutorials to launch junk removal side hustles or full-time businesses. There are around 10,000 junk removal businesses across the U.S., according to the Better Business Bureau. They range in scale from independent side hustles to national companies like 1-800-GOT-JUNK, which touts more than 150 franchise locations.

Other times, Stevens finds himself neck deep in rubbish, ducking concerned security guards, peeling away actual trash and rotting food in the hope of uncovering discarded treasure, he says. He tries to avoid sneaking around and keeps an eye out for "no trespassing" signs. He'd rather leave items behind if a property owner or security guard tells him to vacate the premises, he says.

Even Stevens expresses surprise at how much money he's made, saying he doesn't necessarily think his annual income is sustainable. When he "stumbled into" dumpster diving, he was approximately $12,000 in debt after years of poor spending habits fueled partially by a drug addiction, a stark change from his affluent childhood, he says.

"I dug myself out of a hole. I built up my savings. I really did much more than I thought was possible," says Stevens.

Stevens, a Nashville native, is the son of a five-time Grammy Award-winning producer of country and gospel music who has worked with artists like Blake Shelton and Carrie Underwood. His life took a turn in college when he developed an addiction to prescription Adderall, leading him to drop out partway through his senior year in 2018, he says.

His lack of a college degree made landing full-time work a challenge. He says his parents can afford to support him financially, and they've done so in specific emergency occasions. But mostly, his father wants his kids to learn to fend for themselves, he says: "He will let me sit on the coals for the sake of learning how to swim. That's taught me a lot [and] given me a lot of valuable skills and a tough mentality."

Unemployed, Stevens started combing through thrift shops and Goodwill clothing racks for vintage apparel to sell on eBay. He was inspired by YouTube influencers like Rick Senko, he says, whose Fort Lauderdale, Florida-based clothing resale business Technsports brought in $6.5 million in annual revenue as recently as 2024.

But Stevens' returns were inconsistent, and he often spent his profits on drugs or luxury shopping sprees, he says. After his parents funded a rehab stint in 2021 — helping him "quit doing the hard drugs," he says — he spent his money chasing other dopamine hits. Those indulgences included a $1,200 pair of Christian Dior Birkenstock sandals, an 85-inch flat-screen television and a $30,000 down payment on a Mercedes-AMG sedan that he later lost because he couldn't afford the total cost, he says.

By June 2023, he could no longer afford to pay rent, so he lived in a rented storage unit until the business's security found and evicted him, and then stayed in his Nissan Sentra for three weeks, he says. Homelessness shocked him into learning to manage his "reckless" spending habits, he says: "Being punished by your own spending addiction was a wringer that I promised myself I can't be put through again."

In October 2023, a cousin lent him money to start renting an apartment, and Stevens worked to stick to a sensible enough budget for his thrifting income to cover future rent and basic expenses, he says. Five months later, he found a discarded pair of black Nike Air Force Ones in good condition next to a gas station pump. He took them home, sanitized them and sold them online for $35, he says.

"That was the first time I had ever found something that was trash and sold it and made a little bit of money," says Stevens.

Once he started dumpster diving, Stevens was hooked "because I never stopped finding good stuff in these dumpsters," he says.

His biggest score to date: dozens of discarded Steelcase office chairs, worth $1,000-plus each when new, outside an overflowing office park dumpster, he says. He got his car — by this point, a used pickup truck and a self-built trailer — and hauled away 190 chairs to list online at hundreds of dollars apiece. He sold the majority of them in bulk transactions on Facebook Marketplace for $17,000, and brought in an additional $10,600 selling the remaining chairs individually, documents show.

If Stevens manages to find roughly one valuable haul per month, he can fill his $400-a-month storage unit and start listing the items. For the most part, he scopes out corporate environments, preferring "dry dumpsters" over ones laden with food waste, he says. Office trash is particularly profitable when companies downsize, says Stevens: Once, he found 120 standing desks discarded outside a building.

Near high-end apartment buildings, Stevens has found furniture from brands like West Elm, iPads, electronics and power tools in working condition, he says. "Almost every piece of furniture in my apartment came from a dumpster," says Stevens.

Stevens works alone and minimizes gas costs by sticking to locations within a few miles from his apartment. The majority of his income has come from big hauls across his five highest-earning months, reflecting the venture's hit-or-miss nature. "I got really lucky earlier this year and I made a ton of money. I'm not acting like this is something I could do every year," he says.

So he saves as much of his earnings as possible, "constantly" talking himself out of unnecessary purchases, he says. And he's studying the business of real estate wholesaling — which involves securing contracts for distressed properties and flipping them to investors for a fee — as a potentially more secure way to earn a living, he adds.

Still, he acknowledges the allure of knowing his next big payday could always be one dumpster away.

"I don't see myself ever turning down a good looking dumpster," says Stevens. "I have found so much cool stuff that I feel like I'll always be curious about what's in that dumpster."

#2899. Packers Snap Counts, Week 9: Green Bay shakes up the offensive line

The Green Bay Packers broke the 100-yard rushing mark on Sunday against the New England Patriots, in part because they changed up both their backfield and their offensive line. Will those changes stick moving forward? That hasn't been determined yet.

Let's get into how this Packers team looked different than in the past in our week's snap count breakdown. As a reminder, these only include offensive and defensive snaps, not special teams.

Backup quarterback Tyrod Taylor got in for the second time this season, his first since he kneeled down twice against the Chicago Bears. Taylor operated the offense on the last few drives of the game, when the Packers had already put away the game. No quarterback controversy here.

The leading rusher on the night was Kaleb Johnson, who has been a healthy scratch as of lately. Johnson was pushed into action because of MarShawn Lloyd's knee injury, and he split time on the field about 50-50 once starter Josh Jacobs left the game with a fractured finger.

Between Lloyd, Jacobs and Chris Brooks, who only has two weeks left in his practice window, the Packers will need to make some tough decisions soon at the running back position. They're dealing with three injuries, have five roster-caliber players, can only carry four on the 53-man roster (at most) and can only carry three on the game-day 48-man roster. At some point, something is going to have to give here.

Former practice squadder and current punt returner Coleman Owen got his first looks on offense in garbage time. He had not played on offense up to this point in the season. Owen was also pulled off the kickoff return unit, allowing Bo Melton to be the top returner with J. Michael Sturdivant as the "off-returner."

Belton had a 48-yard return that highlighted the night in the kicking game, while Owen had a 13-yard punt return.

Oh, yeah. Christian Watson and Matthew Golden went off.

There's nothing special about the tight end rotation this week, other than Josh Whyle being out with an injury. Last week, the Packers decided to roster only three tight ends on game day for the first time all season, and they don't seem to want to do that again. Instead, they brought up Drake Dabney from the practice squad to help replace Whyle's snaps on special teams. While Dabney didn't play on offense, he did play six snaps in the kicking game.

The Packers played all eight of their offensive linemen today, with their last two — Mekhi Becton and Jager Burton — only getting in when Tyrod Taylor hit the field. For what it's worth, Becton played left tackle in his reps. In total, 13 offensive linemen have now played for Green Bay's offense this season.

The big story here, though, is that the Packers are back to a guard rotation, which they have been famous for in previous years. This time, though, they're moving around three guards for two spots, not just splitting reps at one spot between two guards.

Before garbage time, Green Bay played two drives where Laken Tomlinson played left guard and Kevin Zeitler played right guard. Aaron Banks was on the bench for those looks. Otherwise, the team played Banks at left guard, pushed Tomlinson at right guard and Zeitler was on the bench. So the Banks vs. Zeitler rotation also comes with Tomlinson flip-flopping sides to get them on the field, too.

For a staff that tried to sell us on continuity on the line early in the year when the guards were at their worst under Jacob Monk and Donovan Jennings, they seem to have thrown that playbook out entirely. A guard musical chairs is going on, and we've already seen six players suit up at those two spots this season.

Who even knows which eight players are going to be up on game days when Sean Rhyan and Travis Glover are finally back healthy? I'm done guessing about who the line will be. We'll find out on Sundays.

Again, nothing too shocking here. Campbell and Riley are going to be the lower defensive linemen on the team's depth chart until the likes of Warren Brinson and/or Jonathan Ford can return from the injured reserve.

It remains close to an even split for all four of the Packers' top edge defenders, something we've seen throughout the year. Can this keep up whenever Micah Parsons returns to the lineup? I doubt it.

After just two defensive snaps, injury replacement starter Ty'Ron Hopper left the game with a chest injury, which head coach Matt LaFleur was not asked about in the post-game press conference. Officially, the Packers ruled him out of action, and he never returned to the game.

At the end of the game, Isaiah McDuffie, the team's replacement starter at the Mike position, left with an undisclosed injury, too. Forcing Kristian Welch, the team's sixth linebacker option, into the game.

Keep an eye on the injury report this week, as the Packers cannot afford to be out Zaire Franklin, Edgerrin Cooper, McDuffie and Hopper. If those four can't go, they might need to call up practice squad linebacker Jack Dingle and sign a player off the street.

Carrington Valentine got one rep as a dime defender (a second slot) in the first half, but otherwise his snaps and Benjamin St-Juste's snaps came in garbage time, when Keisean Nixon and Brandon Cisse were pulled out of the game. Kamal Hadden's snaps all game as a slot defender, when safety Javon Bullard was getting worked on.

Kitan Oladapo played some safety in garbage time and when Evan Williams was out. Williams had the air knocked out of him at one point, but he returned to action.

At the end of the game, it was Hadden in the slot with Williams and Oladapo playing the high safety looks, due to their lack of bodies at the position. Ultimately, the team allowed Xavier McKinney and Javon Bullard, who was in and out of the game twice, to rest.

#2898. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2896. Knicks Bulletin: ‘I wasn’t lying in that sense’

The Cavaliers beat the Knicks on Saturday, adding another win to their analytical spreadsheet.

Cleveland remains undefeated on that front, while New York keeps stacking dubs on the court.

On giving Landry Shamet more leeway than younger reserves and keeping bench minutes competitive:

"Obviously, you looking at a guy like Landry, he may have a little bit more leash than Pac [Dadiet]. Landry's as close to being a starter as you can imagine for us. Everybody else, they have to play and even the starters and Landry, they do, too."

On Landry Shamet's standing in the Knicks' rotation:

"About as close to being a starter as you can imagine for us."

On choosing Tyler Kolek over Jose Alvarado as the backup point guard:

"But you know, Jose had the first opportunity [against the Nets) and then I gave Tyler an opportunity in the second half and Tyler played well. So I go with Tyler and if I feel I need to make a change, Tyler's not getting it done in my eyes, I'll go back to Jose."

On Josh Hart's ability to contribute without having plays called for him:

"Josh is a connector. I don't think we call a single play for him at any time, he just goes and gets it done.

"He rebounds. Our pick and roll defense has been bad, and he's helped clean it up at times by putting him on the ball. So his versatility defensively is a bonus for us. So he's definitely going to be missed."

On facing Cleveland again after defeating the Cavaliers in the Eastern Conference finals:

"I mean, they're a great team. We saw them in the Eastern Conference finals. Obviously, they have another year under their belt with each other, more experience. And we saw what experience does for teams, especially like us."

On acknowledging that he underestimated Taylor Swift's history as a Knicks fan:

"Obviously with everything (going on), people have been going back and sending me all the stuff about her going to the games. And she was actually wearing a 13 jersey (in one picture). Probably Joakim Noah back in the day with Carmelo (Anthony). So, I wasn't up to date on my Taylor Swift Knicks fandom, so that's on me."

On the reaction to his comments about Taylor Swift and admitting he answered the question poorly:

"(The video) was everywhere. Everybody saw it. But it is what it is. For me, it's just a question that was asked to me that maybe I answered in the wrong way. I take that on the chin. But I love her music. We were just playing it five minutes ago and I'm singing every word to every song. Nothing against her at all."

On being a Taylor Swift fan despite questioning her Knicks fandom:

"I love her music. I probably know more songs than anybody else in the locker room. I just misspoke."

On returning to Cleveland and remembering the Knicks' championship run:

"It's human nature, you're gonna revert back and think back to the last time you were in the building. I had a quick little firing of that walking in for shootaround, but I think the general sense was that was last year. That was great. You can have those memories and hang on to that as long as we want, but ultimately this year is a new year and a new endeavor."

On the benefits of roster continuity and familiarity with Mike Brown:

"The biggest thing is just being comfortable with the guys on the floor, second year with [head coach] Mike [Brown]. I think that just breeds confidence. Familiarity breeds confidence. There's no denying that. It allows you to get to the point of conversations quicker. It makes for a good formula to find success."

On comparing his unpredictable Knicks role to that of a baseball relief pitcher:

"Especially with [my] role, I think I take a lot from my buddy Ashby, bullpen guy, you don't know when you're gonna get thrown in, what the situation's gonna be. My job might look a little different from night to night. If I'm changing my approach every night, that's not setting myself up for a good situation.

"I think in the past in my career when I was younger I would try to overcomplicate things and think I have to reinvent the wheel. I try to just keep it as simple as possible, reduce it down to my job, my impact and just play my game."

On Cleveland's Saturday loss to the Knicks:

"No excuse. These are games that we feel like we should win. We got another tough stretch coming up so there's no reason to sit here and feel bad and nothing's won in the first 10 games."

"I think we just gave them too many second-chance opportunities. Then it's too late, you're down 20 trying to fight back, but it's too late."

On the Cavaliers failing to match the Knicks' intensity:

"Got to bring your A game and we just didn't do that tonight."

On Cleveland continuing to compete despite trailing New York by 18 points:

"We could have folded down 18, could have packed it in on a back-to-back, tough week, but we kept at it."

On acknowledging that the Knicks dominated the Cavaliers in the Eastern Conference finals and then won the championship:

"They dominated it, and they did it again against San Antonio [in the Finals]. Analytically … there's an 'expected score,' and we've won two out of the three games.

"I think when you look back on it and take the context of the question, I'm not sure that was taken into account, which I know was a little bit of a lesson for me — like, you can just take a sound bite (and run with it).

"I would just say there was no coach more complimentary of the Knicks and honest about our performance and how they dominated us. So you live and learn a little bit with those things. But like I said, 99 percent of my comments were how they dominated us. They were by far the better team. I answered the question, as it was taken, (which was) 'And you're down 3-0, what hope do you have of even giving to your guys?'

"We get a lot of information … and I wasn't lying in that sense, but in no sense was it like, 'Oh, man, we deserve to be winning.' Like zero — zero — thought of that."

On why analytics could not outweigh the Knicks' physical and mental dominance:

"We get information that checks our process. All that being said, we were still dominated. It kind of doesn't matter (what the analytics say) because there is more than that. There is the mental toughness, the physical toughness, the flow to the game, how aggressive you are. There are all these other intangibles that go with that."

On recognizing that the Knicks earned their championship:

"They deserved it, and they did it again against San Antonio. Just an incredible, incredible run."

#2894. Jordan Love, Packers offense give Patriots' $135 million cornerback nightmares

The passes arc with a sense of predetermination these days when Jordan Love uncorks another deep ball.

The intended target is certainly going to have a chance to catch the ball, and odds are he has some degree of separation against the doomed coverage. What's less of a guarantee is whether the pass catcher will perform his duty and complete the grab, but that was not an issue during the Packers' 45-27 victory over the New England Patriots on Sunday, Nov. 8.

The numbers are cartoonish, as Green Bay put up a season high in points and total yards (478) – the worst rushing team in the NFL entering the week scored three times on the ground and ran for 129 yards with running back Josh Jacobs sidelined for most of the game after suffering a broken finger.

Love finished with 372 yards passing and three scores through the air and added a rushing touchdown to kick off the scoring 101 seconds into the game to create a well-balanced stat sheet. The one-two punch in the wide-receiver room of Christian Watson and Matthew Golden showed out with a combined 17 receptions on 18 targets for 335 yards and two touchdowns.

"It's bombs away," NBC's Jason Garrett said during "Football Night in America."

Fellow NBC analyst Mike Tomlin said it was time to consider the tandem among the elite duos in the league "at the tip-top of the food chain."

The performance put Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel in position to criticize one of his best players in cornerback Christian Gonzalez. Signed to a four-year, $135 million extension that made him one of the richest corners in the NFL, Gonzalez was targeted by Love 11 times. He was successful on 10 of those occasions, for 187 yards and a touchdown.

"When your best players don't play well, it's hard to win in this league," Vrabel said.

The 2025 Coach of the Year continued: "We expect a lot more. He expects a lot more. We need more from him and everybody else. It wasn't just him. Again, we got to be able to coach and be prepared, and then we have to execute. Just like it is every single week when we don't win, it's all of us, and it starts with me."

By the end of the first quarter, Golden had 126 receiving yards. Love completed seven passes that went for 24 yards or more and averaged 12.8 yards per attempt. He was 6-for-8 on deep passes (20+ air yards) for 235 yards and a touchdown, according to Next Gen Stats.

It marked the second time Love completed six deep passes this season (Week 5 against the Chicago Bears was the other) – no other quarterback in the NFL has done it once in 2026. Love said it felt like he was "flowing" while dropping back and throwing.

"It feels like you're in that flow state and just finding ways to get guys the ball ... it's a great feeling, and you just want to keep chasing every play and keep making good plays," Love told reporters. "I think I made some good throws, but I think guys are making pretty good catches as well. We've just got to keep building on that. Christian and 'MG,' I looked at their stat line after the game, it was just crazy. It's crazy to see that, and I think they're balling right now. So we've just got to keep finding ways to get them the rock. "

More: Tom Brady 'shocked' to learn mom of Green Bay Packer was his babysitter

Golden tagged Gonzalez for seven catches and 105 yards, while Watson handed him 82 yards over three catches, per Next Gen Stats. A single defender surrendering more than 80 yards to a pair of teammates in one game has not happened since at least 2018, also per Next Gen Stats.

LaFleur said any appearance of going after Gonzalez was more related to attacking specific coverages.

"When you got a quarterback playing the way Jordan Love's playing," said LaFleur, who appeared eager to widen the margin of victory against Vrabel, his former boss with the Tennessee Titans. "(I'm) just dialing up the plays.

"Just calling it. I can't say enough about it. Those are low-percentage throws in theory, but he's making them look high-percentage. I mean, they're right on the money."

Love left Gillette Stadium, where Tom Brady called his first Patriots game as a broadcaster, to chants of "MVP!" from visiting and well-traveled Packers fans.

"It's definitely cool," Love said of the recognition, "but just got to keep going and really just block everything else out and keep focusing on what I can do as an individual and to be able to help our team just keep getting better."

#2891. Mark Cuban says he doesn’t ‘give a f---' about growing his net worth—a healthy mindset for non-billionaires too, a financial therapist says

Mark Cuban has zero F's to give — at least, when it comes to his net worth. Financial therapists say that's a healthy mindset to have, even if you're not a multibillionaire like Cuban.

"I don't give a f--- about my net worth. I have enough. My family has enough," Cuban posted Monday on social media platform X, responding to another user's post about the growth of the serial entrepreneur's wealth going back to 1990.

"I don't care about moving up on the Forbes 400," Cuban added.

Cuban currently has an estimated net worth of $6.8 billion, according to Forbes, which ranks the former star of ABC's "Shark Tank" as the 597th wealthiest person in the world. Cuban sold his first business, a software company called MicroSolutions, for $6 million in 1990. He became a billionaire when he and his co-founders sold Broadcast.com to Yahoo for $5.7 billion in 1999.

He developed his attitude after buying a majority stake in the NBA's Dallas Mavericks in January 2000, he tells CNBC Make It: "I was more interested in winning than making money." The mindset might seem obvious to some: After all, why would a billionaire have any stress about his bank account? But even people with lots of money experience financial stress and anxiety, studies have shown.

What seems to set Cuban apart from some other wealthy people is the confidence he exhibits in how he puts his wealth to use, says Christine Hargrove, an Athens, Georgia-based financial therapist. In his post, Cuban wrote that he doesn't obsess over growing his personal net worth because, in part, he'd rather see the impact his money can have on the world.

"My next dollar can impact other people's lives a lot more than mine," wrote Cuban, citing his decision to launch the cost-cutting pharmacy startup Cost Plus Drugs and his many small-business investments across 15 seasons on "Shark Tank" as an investor judge. "That is what drives my investments. I look for ways to have an impact."

Confidence in how you save or spend your money is often a signal of a healthy financial mindset, notes Hargrove, who is president-elect of the Financial Therapy Association and Assistant Director of the Love and Money Center at the University of Georgia.

"You actually have to be the person to decide what is enough for you," she says, adding: "Having a sense of purpose with your money and with your life can be an incredible source of protection against chronic financial anxiety."

Many Americans, especially as costs of living remain high nationwide, have no choice but to worry about their finances.

When those with plenty of money in their bank accounts or stock portfolios obsess over their level of wealth, they're also probably not doing so by choice, notes Hargrove. Some people's financial insecurities come from growing up in low-income households. Others may think of themselves as relatively struggling because they earn less than the other wealthy people around them.

"Having a healthy relationship with money isn't just about how much you have. It's about understanding what money means to you and what you want it to do for you," says Cara Macksoud, an Orlando-based accredited financial counselor and financial behavior specialist.

No matter what your financial situation is, anxiety about money can actually hurt your finances in addition to the toll it takes on your mental health. Financial stress and related mental health challenges can hurt workplace performance through distraction and absenteeism, according to a 2024 report by the TIAA Institute, the research arm of retirement fund manager TIAA. Poor mental health can also impair cognition, making it more difficult to make sound money decisions, the report suggested.

Obsessing over every dollar can also make it feel impossible to part with money for causes that would otherwise align with your personal values, such as charitable giving or support for loved ones, Hargrove says. "And then our money actually comes between us and who we want to be," she adds.

Financial anxiety often results in repetitive behaviors, such as obsessively checking your bank account or stock portfolio, notes Hargrove. For those not in immediate financial peril, Hargrove suggests reminding yourself "enough is enough" and working to replace those behaviors with actions that foster gratitude for the cherished aspects of your life that are not related to your finances.

If you constantly check banking apps on your phone, you might change your wallpaper to a photo of your family or friend group to remind you of the safety of your family and social networks, for instance. It's a simple trick, but it can soon become part of a pattern of "focusing on thoughts of gratitude" that research has shown can lower levels of the stress hormone cortisol in your body, Hargrove says.

And if you can feel like you're using your money to make a positive difference, whether for yourself or others — as Cuban suggested in his post that he does — you probably have a healthier mindset around money than a lot of other Americans, notes Hargrove.

"Living with gratitude and feeling like you make a difference, either for yourself or for other people, [there's] not much better than that in life, right?" Hargrove says.

CNBC Select is editorially independent and may earn a commission from affiliate partners on links.

#2890. Highlights: Spurs third-quarter barrage blows out Trail Blazers

Coming off an opening NBA Cup win against the Sacramento Kings, the Spurs faced a SEGABABA against the Portland Trail Blazers. After a sluggish first quarter, the Spurs, behind by 42 points in the second quarter, built their two-point lead to 15 at halftime. In the third, the Spurs drove the nail in the coffin, outscoring the Blazers 45-27 and ballooning their lead to 33 heading into the fourth. The Blazers simply could not stop the ball movement and three-point barrage by the silver and black, as the Spurs ultimately won 125-103.

Victor Wembanyama continues his early MVP campaign with another efficient high-scoring game of 29 points (9-13 FG, 3-6 3PT, 8-9 FT), seven rebounds, three assists, a block, and a steal. Despite only playing 24 minutes and not being as big a focal point of the offense as he was against Dallas and Sacramento, Wemby once again demonstrated how unguardable he truly is. After starting the season shooting under 30% from three, Wemby is now shooting 7-12 (58%) from three in his last two games.

Since he's shown he can shoot threes, Donovan Clingan steps out to guard him but immediately gets beat by Wemby on the drive to the basket. All Toumani Camara can do is go straight up, but against a seven-foot-four (he's definitely taller) alien, it's almost pointless.

The bulk of Wemby's scoring comes from inside the paint. He is dunking the ball more than any other player in the league and is even leading other TEAMS in total dunks. Where do most of his dunks come from? Lobs, of course.

The most unguardable lob threat in the league, all his teammates have to do is just throw it up above the rim to where only Wemby can grab it. Sometimes, defenders are in the way. This time, Clingan was unfortunately one of his victims.

Devin Vassell dropped another solid game with 15 points, three assists, two rebounds, and two steals. His defense somehow outshone his shooting from three. His agility and lateral movement allow him to stay in front of multiple guards and forwards. He also deflected multiple passes, and one of them turned into a steal and layup all by himself.

Stephon Castle dropped 14 points, seven rebounds, five assists, and a block. Castle, like Vassell, played like a Swiss Army knife against the Blazers. He crashed the glass, dished out dimes, and had himself some highlight buckets. The hustle play of the game was him somehow tight-roping the sideline and dishing out to an open Vassell for the corner three.

Poor Clingan. De'Aaron Fox and Castle run a beautiful DHO that results in a posterizing alley-oop with Castle dunking on his former college teammate.

Although they didn't shoot 50% from three this game, they still drained 17 compared to Portland's nine. For most Spurs fans, that third-quarter explosion showed that when everyone is bought in and focused within the flow of the offense, this team can put teams away early. The communication also reached the defense, as they held the Blazers to 76 points after 36 minutes (before garbage time). Next week, the Spurs face the Suns, Bulls, Warriors, & Jazz.

Finally, here are the full game highlights.

The Spurs' early road trip continues with a visit to the Mortgage Matchup Center, the home of the Phoenix Suns, this Monday at 8:00 P.M. (CST) on DAZN.

#2886. Another QB injury: Drew Lock’s status ‘up in the air’ for Week 10 vs. Raiders

It might be Jalen Milroe time for the Seattle Seahawks.

While Drew Lock didn't miss any game action during Seattle's 17-3 win over the Arizona Cardinals, he has a new injury on top of the severe tendinitis he reportedly had been battling over the past couple of weeks. Lock was seen grabbing his right hip area during the third quarter of Sunday's game, grimacing and walking gingerly as he got off the field prior to Seattle's final scoring drive. Jalen Milroe warmed up on the sidelines while Lock went into the blue tent, but Lock stayed in for the rest of the afternoon and didn't miss a play.

Seahawks coach Mike Macdonald told reporters at the post-game press conference that Lock's status is "up in the air" after getting his oblique looked at.

"We're looking at him for an oblique, so we'll assess it and go from there," Macdonald said.

"He made it out [of the game], but you know, like I've sat here before and told you a guy would be ready and then he wasn't. So, leave my options open."

For what it's worth, Lock said "we'll get it looked at [Sunday night]" and that it "was not too bad." When asked if the injury affected him, he said, "I wouldn't say it did."

Lock also said he felt "good" playing through the elbow injury.

Odds are not sounding favorable for Sam Darnold to return against the Las Vegas Raiders, with ESPN's Adam Schefter reporting what felt like a pretty obvious situation that he is in "jeopardy" of missing Week 10 with quad and rib injuries. If Darnold was listed as "week-to-week" by Mike Macdonald, this isn't earth-shattering news as much as it affirms what's already been said.

If the news is negative on Lock, then we could see Jalen Milroe's first career NFL start. But at the minimum, this is a very banged up Seahawks quarterback room and the bye week might be one of the most welcome byes in recent Seahawks history.

#2884. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2882. Pistons vs Lakers preview: Best Defense vs best offense

Does it get any better than the No. 1 offense versus the No. 1 defense? The Detroit Pistons find themselves in that matchup as the 122-point-per-game Los Angeles Lakers visit Motown tonight.

Luka Dončić and the Lakers put up plenty of buckets, but scoring on Detroit is still an impossible task. The Pistons only surrender 101 points a game. Smothering defense every night.

Detroit's defense has its biggest test of the season if Dončić suits up this evening. He's questionable with lower back tightness.

It would be a shame if we're robbed of Cade Cunningham going head-to-head with another MVP candidate. The bigger shame would be if we miss out on Dončić versus Ausar Thompson. That matchup is like Jerry Rice versus Deion Sanders with how both are operating.

Detroit hasn't played since Wednesday's loss to the Philadelphia 76ers. They've had plenty of time to rest up. They'll need it if Dončić suits up.

Dončić is averaging 40 points. He's shooting 42% on nearly 14 3s a game. He gets to the line more than anybody. It's basically no stopping Dončić at the moment. This is a Wilt Chamberlain-like hot streak. Those two are the only players to average 40 nine games into the season. Whew, to say he's balling is an understatement.

But tasks like this are why the Pistons employ Ausar Thompson. If *anybody* can slow Dončić down, it's the best perimeter defender on the planet. Dončić usually has success against quick defenders with wiry strength, but Ausar is bigger and stronger this season. We'll be keeping tabs on how Ausar takes Dončić's bumps tonight. Ausar has the quick hands to disrupt things as well.

This might be a game where we see Paul Reed getting to the level and blitzing a bunch. J.B. Bickerstaff may decide to get the ball out of Dončić's hands and force someone else to beat them. Reed is capable of coming to the level on screens and muddying things up.

I'm sure Dončić will see some Cade tonight too. Cade won't make it easy with his sturdy frame. On the other end, the Lakers only have a few perimeter defenders to throw at Cade.

Matisse Thybulle is firmly in LA's rotation as he's started all 10 games. He's still a defensive playmaking threat. Cade deals with defenders like this nightly, but Thybulle is a plus defender nonetheless. So is Ziaire Williams. The springy 25-year-old treats every possession as if he'll never get in the game again if he doesn't go hard.

Those two are solid defensively, and Jake LaRavia competes. Outside of that, Detroit will have plenty of favorable matchups (Walker Kessler is a great rim protector and is off to a good offensive start. He can make a difference).

Dončić displays effort and is trying to shake the terrible defender narrative on his name, but attacking him is still the best way to slow him down. Make him work and keep him involved in actions.

Austin Reaves falls into the same boat. He's under Dončić on the Lakers' best defenders on the team list. Anybody with that matchup should look to be aggressive.

Reaves spends most of his time covering shooting guards. Will JJ Redick make Reaves chase Duncan Robinson all night? That's probably not ideal. Robinson runs even great defenders into the ground.

Robinson's light-em-up stretch continues as he's fourth in 3s made (35). Only Dončić, Nickeil Alexander-Walker, and Steph Curry have made more.

Detroit is letting it fly from deep as a team. According to Cleaning the Glass, 40 percent of their shots are coming from 3 (15th) compared to 31 percent last year (29th). Their rim rate has flipped the other way, but getting Jalen Duren back should rectify that.

The best offense versus the best defense is a mandatory viewing appointment. Can Ausar slow down the MVP-favorite? Do the Pistons show why they're an elite defense and smother Dončić as a team? Does Cade deliver a masterclass against a guy he'll be compared to for the foreseeable future if he's in the MVP race? We'll find out at 6 PM. Be there!

Detroit Pistons (6-2): Cade Cunningham, Duncan Robinson, Ausar Thompson, John Collins, Paul Reed

Los Angeles Lakers (8-2): Luka Dončić, Austin Reaves, Matisse Thybulle, Jake LaRavia, Walker Kessler

What are your thoughts on Austin Reaves? He reportedly did not want to be drafted by Detroit. Does that still bother you?

#2878. Patriots’ best players fail to deliver in blowout loss to Packers

NFL teams are only as good as their best players.

For the New England Patriots, that is quarterback Drake Maye and cornerback Christian Gonzalez. But during Sunday's 45-27 loss to the Green Bay Packers, neither played up to expectations.

"When your best players don't play good, you don't have much of a chance in this league. So, we expect a lot more," head coach Mike Vrabel said.

Gonzalez struggled against Packers wide receivers Matthew Golden and Christian Watson. After never allowing more than 71 yards in a game to a single receiver, both Packers wideouts went for 80-plus yards with Gonzalez in coverage.

In total, Gonzalez surrendered a career-high 10 receptions for 187 receiving yards on Sunday after he allowed just 196 yards in coverage through his first six games this season.

"Obviously, it is frustrating. I want to be who I am for the team, and everybody that knows what I am. But, it's football. I'm going to have a bad game," Gonzalez said. "So, it's the same as I come up here week in, week out. If it's a good game, I say, 'All right. We move on.' It's just like that."

Vrabel added: "We expect a lot more. He expects a lot more. We need more from him and everybody else. It wasn't just him."

On the offensive side of the ball, Maye was not his usual self under center.

His first pass of the afternoon was intercepted by Packers safety Xavier McKinney, setting up a Green Bay touchdown just two plays later. The turnover put New England in an early 7-0 hole less than two minutes into the game.

"I'm disappointed in myself for, you can't put the team in the hole straight off the bat, first possession, first half," Maye said.

That was a lead the Packers never surrendered, as Maye threw two additional interceptions in the loss, one that he finished on the bench.

"I feel like in the times we had chances to get back into the game, I messed them up, and we had chances to put us back within three points multiple times, and just frustrating," Maye said. "I got to do a better job from a whole perspective, communication, doing the little things right, and I just, I wasn't good. I wasn't good today. And we got to bounce back."

#2876. What we learned from the Spurs’ win over the Trail Blazers

If you've been looking for a signature San Antonio Spurs win early in the 2026-27 season, look no further. On the second game of a back-to-back, the Spurs waltzed into enemy territory and blew out a team motivated to get revenge. It was one of the first times we've seen this year's Spurs put it all together against a playoff-caliber team.

Defense was the name of the game in San Antonio's 125-103 win over the Portland Trail Blazers. Both teams played incredibly physical (or just flopped a lot). But the Spurs were the enforcers overall. Stephon Castle set the tone on the perimeter with some suffocating defense on two of the Blazers' best players, Deni Advija and Jrue Holiday. Advija was particularly frustrated by Castle's defense, scoring just 15 points on 5-13 shooting.

The rest of the team forced turnovers. Portland coughed it up 18 times, leading to 23 easy points for the Spurs. The tape will show that the Spurs dominated this game with force and athleticism. The stat sheet shows they won a lot of the hustle stats, too. San Antonio won the rebounding battle by 13 and grabbed 14 offensive boards. The force they showed against Portland is the same as what they showed last season in their NBA Finals run, but more refined.

The Blazers just feel like a team the Spurs will have to beat in the Victor Wembanyama era. They are young (for the most part), tough, and skilled. Beating them down in such a dominant fashion after the playoff victory last season is a clear sign that San Antonio has not taken a step back, despite some bumps to start the season. It also showed a lot of grit and edge from the Spurs. It was exactly the type of victory this team needed early in the season.

I gave the second unit a tough time in the last WWL. They were MUCH better against Portland. Luke Kornet in particular had one of his best games of the season with 6 points, 4 rebounds, 2 assists, and a block. There were fewer moments when Kornet needed to go one-on-one with a driver at the rim, as the perimeter defense made it harder for the Blazers to get clear drives to the basket. What was even more impressive about Kornet's game is that he actually took a few shots when he was open. Typically, the backup center isn't even thinking about shooting, but he hit a pop-a-shot early in the game and took a jumper from the free-throw line that didn't fall. Just getting Kornet to take those kinds of shots when they are available will help the second-unit offense tremendously.

It's been a minute since we got a sub-25-minute elite performance from Wembanyama. Not because the performances haven't been elite, but because he's usually playing more than 25 minutes. He had 29 points on 9-for-13 shooting, including three triples. Portland had absolutely no answer for the early-season MVP candidate. In the last two games, he's become more selective with his jump shots, and the results have been better looks that are going in more often.

Carter Bryant got an opportunity to play early in the game and made the most of it. He hit a pair of three-pointers, got a deflection and a block, providing energy off the bench. Bryant still struggles with overaggressiveness on defense. He picked up 5 fouls in just 17 minutes on Saturday. Bryant has a lot of talent; it just needs to be harnessed with improved awareness and experience.

The Blazers continued their strategy of guarding Castle with Donovan Clingan and putting Toumani Camara on Wembanyama. It's safe to say the Spurs have figured out how to defeat that coverage. It helps that Castle made two of his five three-pointers, and Wembanyama is a game-breaker. Portland still needed to send doubles at Wembanyama, and when they did, the Spurs were able to get easy looks when the ball was passed out.

#2874. Cathie Wood 'always feels better when the market is worried' — here's why

Ark Invest CEO Cathie Wood is shrugging off this week's market jitters, telling CNBC on Friday that concerns among investors actually reinforce her optimism that further gains lie ahead.

U.S. equities sold off sharply on Thursday, reversing the initial rally seen in the wake of the midterm elections. AI-related stocks led the rout, sending the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite into correction territory. On Friday, tech stocks staged a tentative recovery.

Much of the sell-off on Thursday was driven by concerns that a Democrat-led Congress would lead to a slowdown in data center construction. But concerns about artificial intelligence – particularly when it comes to capital expenditure, cash flow, debt and circular financing – have sparked multiple bouts of volatility over the course of this year.

Wood, whose firm manages various funds focused on tech and innovation, told CNBC there was a "wall of worry" pervading financial markets right now. But her decades of experience as an investor give her a somewhat unconventional view of the state of play.

"I experienced the tech and telecom bubble, and you know there really wasn't much worry about it," she said. "It was the 80s and the 90s, and it was a bull market, and people were just assuming this would go to the moon, that is not how this [the AI trade] has evolved."

She explained that "from the beginning," many investors have been concerned about how much firms are spending on AI development, how rapidly the spending is taking place, and how the hyperscalers have moved from extreme levels of positive free cash flow to negative free cash flow.

"So it's been unsettling the markets for quite some time," she said. "I always feel better when the market is worried, because that tends to suggest a more durable bull market, and I don't think we've derailed this bull market."

In 2025, her flagship Ark Innovation ETF gained more than 35%, far outperforming the S&P 500's 16.4% rise and the Nasdaq Composite's 20.5% rally.

Asked on Friday whether markets were anywhere near the end of the current bull run, Wood replied: "I don't think so."

"I think we're in the middle of a technology revolution," she said, pointing to the development of technologies like robotaxis and AI agents.

One thing that did risk becoming a "showstopper," Wood noted, was the rising cost of capital.

Interest rates and bond yields have risen sharply this year as the U.S.-Iran war pushes inflation higher across the globe. Yields on corporate bonds have been rising too, with creditors demanding greater compensation for lending to companies.

"This correction is partly caused by what's going on in the bond markets," Wood said on Friday.

"I had been saying for quite some time that the wall of worry has been built by senior leaders in the financial world who were young during the bubble," she said.

"It's the elder statesmen and women of the industry who are keeping this wall of worry in place, and that's not bad. Now you can add the bond investor to that. Bond investors in the old days weren't necessary for technology. It was software. It was low fixed cost. It was viral apps, very high margins. [AI] is high fixed cost, so bond investors are experiencing or taking a journey into the technology world, really that they haven't very much before."

But she noted that AI hyperscalers were somewhat insulated by opportunities for growth.

"If a company thinks that taking on debt is going to accelerate its momentum, meaning its growth rate, it hardly matters what happens to interest rates for those companies," Wood explained.

Wood said her advice to investors in the current volatile environment was to identify their high-conviction positions and double down on them as the market shifts.

"When you see a rally in a volatile environment, take some profits. When you see a sell-off, you'll have the psychological wherewithal if you have taken profits to move back in," she said.

"That's what we do. So what we do during periods like this is concentrate towards our highest conviction names. So that's what we've been doing in the last week or so. Our SpaceX position has gone up. If anything, the controversy around data centers on Earth is giving data centers in space a bigger opportunity."

In 2027, Wood said she expects the equity rally to continue.

"This is two steps forward, one step back," she said of the post-election turbulence. "The revolution will become clearer and clearer next year, and I think the market will do well."

#2873. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2869. How Democrats will try to end Trump's tariffs

The battle for Congress is over. The war over President Donald Trump's tariffs is about to heat up.

The knotted web of tariffs that Trump spent the last two years weaving could soon face its biggest threat, as Democrats prepare to unload their new legislative firepower against the Republican president's signature policy.

Democrats have made no secret of their plans to bombard Trump and his associates with investigations as soon as the next Congress is sworn in. They are also preparing a first-100-hours legislative blitz, putting forward a series of bills that would unwind much of Trump's agenda.

Both prongs of attack will take aim at Trump's unprecedented use of tariffs, which Democrats have quickly identified as a main target following their landslide victory in Tuesday's midterm elections.

Ending Trump's tariff regime, and especially his trade war against Canada, is something Democrats "absolutely have to do immediately," Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed, D-Mich., told MS NOW's "Morning Joe" Thursday.

Rep. Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the Democratic minority leader who could be elected House speaker in the next Congress, has made clear that ending Trump's tariffs is a top priority.

"We should restore congressional authority as it relates to tariffs, because the Trump tariffs are making life more expensive for the American people," he said at a press conference in late August.

If picked as speaker, Jeffries would speed legislation to the House floor that reasserts congressional authority over trade.

Democrats are also laying the groundwork for investigations into Trump's tariffs, which the party folded into its winning campaign message linking high costs with alleged Trumpworld corruption.

Rep. Jamie Raskin of Maryland, the top Democrat on the House Judiciary Committee, on Oct. 28 asked Trump to hand over records related to tariff exemptions. Trump, Raskin alleges, has wielded the exemption power to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

The plans are likely to ratchet up the pressure, both legally and politically, that Trump's tariffs were already facing.

The administration is already embroiled in multiple lawsuits challenging different parts of the government's tariff policy.

The most recent suit, filed Oct. 20, targets Trump's use of a Great Depression-era law to impose 50% tariffs on a range of Canadian goods.

Another court case accuses Trump of trying to recreate the near-global "liberation day" tariffs that the Supreme Court struck down as illegal in late February. The administration has since been ordered to refund $166 billion in tariff revenue it had collected under the scrapped policy.

Congress, not the president, holds the power to levy taxes under the U.S. Constitution. Over decades, the legislative branch has ceded some of those powers to the executive. But Trump has asserted virtually unchecked tariff powers – which Democrats say they are intent on clawing back.

"Every day Trump insists on his indiscriminate taxes on so many of the products Americans need, he digs a deeper hole for Republicans enabling his tariff spree," said Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, who is poised to lead the Senate Finance Committee when Democrats take it over.

"If Democrats somehow had a veto-proof majority, or if voters shame Republicans into doing the right thing, there would be no shortage of trade policies to address," Wyden said in a statement to CNBC before Election Day.

"One of the most obvious priorities would be repealing Trump's tariffs on Canada, which are raising prices and harming communities on both sides of the border," he said. "I'll also continue to work to ensure Congress has the final say on tariffs, so future presidents can't reenact Trump's economically disastrous trade taxes."

The Wall Street Journal, citing Wyden, reported Thursday that Democrats are eyeing the Canada trade war as the "first target" in their efforts against his tariff agenda.

As Wyden's statement suggested, the Senate's rules will hamper Democrats' anti-tariff efforts.

Despite their blowout victory Tuesday night, the party will still lack a 60-vote filibuster-proof majority in the Senate, giving Republicans a crucial tool to block legislation even in their diminished state.

Trump is also poised to lean heavily on his veto pen in the next Congress, presenting a major obstacle for Democrats' legislative priorities.

Overriding a presidential veto requires two-thirds majorities in both the House and Senate, meaning that even with their historic electoral gains, Democrats would need significant Republican help to force a bill into law over Trump's objection.

Democrats may consider it worthwhile to pass legislation even if Trump vetoes it, as doing so could help them build political narratives in the 2028 election cycle. But they may have other options available.

"Absent the brute force of a veto-proof coalition, Congress might consider other political solutions, such as adding riders on 'must-pass' bills or negotiation with the administration," Kit Johnson, a senior tax manager for BDO's customs and international trade group, told CNBC in an email.

Trump has said he plans to use the veto power to force Democrats to the negotiating table. But when it comes to tariffs, it's hard to imagine what deal Trump might accept.

Trump has tried to reshape U.S. trade, foreign and economic policy around his asserted power to slap steep duties on imported goods. He describes tariffs as a win-win, praising them as both a powerful negotiating tool and a major revenue generator.

After Tuesday's election, Trump seemed to suggest tariffs were non-negotiable.

"If I took tariffs off — we have 3,000 plants under construction right now. Every single plant would stop building," Trump told CNBC's Eamon Javers in a phone call Wednesday.

Since taking office in January 2025, Trump has hiked tariffs on nearly every country on the planet, leading to multiple protracted trade wars, most notably against Canada and China. He insists Americans do not share the burden of the duties, even though tariffs by definition are taxes on foreign goods paid by those importing them. The nonprofit Tax Foundation estimates Trump's tariffs have raised the average U.S. household's tax burden by $820 in 2026.

Trump has also consistently overstated the amount of revenue the government has amassed through his tariffs. He claimed before Election Day that tariffs enabled him to offer every U.S. adult $5,000 "dividend" checks if Republicans were to win both chambers of Congress – a plan that would cost roughly $1.2 trillion. But the duties – which are collected from U.S. importers – have only brought in around $300 billion in total between January 2025 and July 2026, according to the Penn Wharton Budget Model.

What's more, about $138 billion of that total has been refunded since the Supreme Court ruling in February. Nearly $20 billion in tariff collections are still set to be refunded.

Most voters do not share Trump's rosy view of tariffs. Sixty-five percent of respondents to the CNBC Voter Poll on Nov. 3 said the duties have hurt the U.S. economy, while just 22% say they helped.

What remains to be seen is how hard Republicans in Congress will push back on Democrats' efforts.

A number of GOP lawmakers came out against the duties during the campaign, as their opponents gained ground blaming inflation on Trump's tariffs and the Iran war.

As they try to regroup and look forward to the 2028 election cycle, some Republicans – who largely supported free trade prior to Trump's political rise – may be less willing to continue aligning themselves with Trump's aggressive protectionism.
