#2867. Christian Gonzalez, Patriots cornerbacks have no answers for Packers passing attack

The New England Patriots are spending $57.4 million in cash on their cornerback room in 2026, according to Spotrac, leading the NFL in spending at the position by a considerable margin.

Given that investment, that group should be among New England's most dependable units. The position accounts for 22.8% of the team's active roster spending.

But that wasn't the case on Sunday against the Green Bay Packers, who marched into Gillette Stadium and handily defeated the Patriots 45-27.

Green Bay's biggest weapon in the victory?

Matthew Golden and Christian Watson each notched career highs in receiving yards, combining for 335 and two touchdowns on 17 receptions. They concluded the game with 164 and 171 yards, respectively.

At the helm of the offense, Packers quarterback Jordan Love completed 23 of his passes, totaling 372 yards and three scores. Just six of his attempts fell incomplete.

New England's defense gave up six explosive plays of 20 or more yards through the air, which head coach Mike Vrabel referred to as the "X-plays" when speaking with the media.

"Just the X-plays," Vrabel said. "When you know that [Green Bay] leads the league in X-play passes. And that's a huge key. That's the difference in the game."

Christian Gonzalez, who received a four-year, $135 million contract extension this past summer, was Love's go-to target in the Patriots' secondary. And that strategy proved successful.

According to PFF, Gonzalez allowed nine catches on 10 targets for 177 yards and a touchdown.

"When your best players don't play good, you don't have much of a chance in this league," Vrabel said. "So, we expect a lot more. [Gonzalez] expects a lot more. We need more from him and everybody else. It wasn't just him. Again, we have to be able to coach and be prepared, and then we have to execute."

The Packers' air raid began early in the first quarter. From their own 17-yard line, Love connected with Golden on back-to-back passes for 65 yards, moving Green Bay into the red zone. Two plays later, Gonzalez appeared to intercept Love, targeting Golden again, but the Patriots' 2023 first-round draft selection was flagged for defensive pass interference.

"They did a good job. [Golden] came out and ran good routes … Love was able to get him the ball," Gonzalez said. "I just have to be able to finish and make those plays … So, it's not the end of the world. I had a bad game. I'm not shaken up by it. I'm not too worried about it. I'll move on and be ready for next week."

Love, now fielding a snap five yards out from the end zone, delivered a touchdown pass to a wide-open Golden after a coverage bust in New England's secondary. Gonzalez and Marcus Jones, matched with Golden and Watson in a two-receiver set, couldn't cover Golden's quick slant as Watson picked both cornerbacks. The two seemingly anticipated Watson running the inside route rather than Golden.

"That's just on me," Gonzalez said, taking responsibility for the breakdown. "I need to be better at talking to [Jones] and getting communication to him faster so we can be on the same page."

Golden totaled 70 yards on that drive before extending the Packers' lead to 14-0.

Vrabel pointed to contributions on both sides of the ball that helped the Patriots make the matchup competitive before halftime. Trailing 17-0 in the second frame, their defense forced consecutive quick punts before Drake Maye connected with Rhamondre Stevenson for a touchdown to trim the deficit.

However, Green Bay quickly answered with a three-play, 69-yard scoring drive led by Watson's 43-yard catch that set up Kaleb Johnson's short rushing score. Despite Carlton Davis being called for another pass interference penalty, Watson still came down with the big gain as safety Kevin Byard, attempting to make a play from the middle of the field, could only watch the play unfold.

"We just didn't execute enough," Byard said. "We knew that was going to be the key to the game, eliminating the big play, and we didn't do good enough today at all. We all have to be better."

Green Bay's success on explosive plays continued in the second half. Leading 34-14 nearing the end of the third frame, Love connected deep with Watson again, logging a 39-yard touchdown, capping a one-play series.

New England, dropping to 5-4 on the season, still dealing with a laundry list of injuries, heads to Munich, Germany, to face off against the 6-2 Detroit Lions on Sunday, Nov. 15.

"Of course it upsets you," Gonzalez said. "We're competitors. All we do is compete … I know that the team counts on me. I know that the defense counts on me. So, I mean, yeah, it hurts to let them down. But like I said, it's football … I had a bad game. I'll watch it and be ready for Detroit."

#2860. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2856. Democrats amp up Trump investigation threats, Blanche counters he may probe lawmakers

Top Democrats are ramping up oversight of the Trump administration and businesses it has partnered with, while Attorney General Todd Blanche warned he could counterinvestigate lawmakers conducting probes.

Democrats romped to majorities in both the House and the Senate during Tuesday's midterm elections, putting them in position to seize control of committee gavels and subpoena power in January. The electoral rout leaves Democrats with at least 54 Senate seats and 254 seats in the House of Representatives as votes continue to be counted in several states.

Democrats vowed to investigate both President Donald Trump's White House and businesses that have worked with the administration. CNBC reported on Thursday that Democrats are expected to take an aggressive approach to probing businesses, especially those companies with ties to the Trump family. The technology, cryptocurrency and prediction market industries, among others, could immediately be in the crosshairs.

"If you've been making your profits by cheating American families and scamming your investors, you better get ready for some investigations — serious ones," said Sen. Elizabeth Warren, the incoming chair of the Senate Banking Committee, on CBS' "Face the Nation" on Sunday. "The American people are just damn sick of this."

Blanche, Trump's former personal attorney, whom the Senate narrowly confirmed as the nation's top cop earlier this year, took umbrage at the Democrats' plans for investigations and said he may launch his own investigations against members of Congress.

"There could be," Blanche said Sunday when pressed on CNN's "State of the Union" about whether the administration will counterinvestigate Democrats.

"When the threats become so vile and so dangerous that you are telling people in college that they better be careful if they want to work in the Trump administration because somebody will come after them, at some point you have to draw the line."

Blanche was referring to comments made by Sen. Elissa Slotkin, D-Mich., who said during a University of Chicago event that she wants "young people coming out of school to say, 'Should I go work for the Trump administration?'...I want them to be like, 'Lord, that might be buying myself a ton of problems that I don't want. And so let me avoid participating in that.' I want that deterrence message sent."

Trump has ordered the Justice Department to investigate Democrats over the comments.

Blanche also took aim at Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., the likely incoming chair of the House Oversight Committee — appearing to caution lawmakers who sit on the panel. Garcia warned the administration and business leaders to "lawyer up" after the election.

"I would be very worried if I was a member on that committee," Blanche said.

Garcia shot back, saying on CNN: "What Todd Blanche is doing right now is a threat."

"He's trying to stop us from doing real oversight," Garcia said. "He is the person that should be reading the Constitution, because the Constitution makes it very clear that it's our job in Congress to conduct oversight of the executive and the administration."

#2855. Jalen Duren will miss at least another 2 weeks due to hamstring injury, Pistons report

The Detroit Pistons provided an update on center Jalen Duren's hamstring injury on Sunday, stating they'll re-evaluate him in another two weeks, though he has been cleared for on-court basketball activity.

If Detroit sticks to that timeline, it would be Sunday, Nov. 22, when the Pistons play the Atlanta Hawks in the first game of a quick two-game road trip.

Duren initially suffered the injury on Friday, Oct. 23, against the Miami Heat. He picked it up in the second quarter and did not finish the game. He's now missed seven consecutive games with the injury.

Without Duren in the lineup, the Pistons are 4-2 and most recently lost 112-106 to the Philadelphia 76ers. If Duren returns in two weeks, he will be back just in time for Detroit to face the Atlanta Hawks on the road on Nov. 22.

The Detroit center has played in just two games so far this season, averaging 13 points, 7.5 rebounds and 2 assists per game. The former first-round pick was named an NBA All-Star for the first time in his career last year after managing career highs in points (19.5), helping lead the Pistons to the No. 1 seed in the Eastern Conference before falling to the Cleveland Cavaliers in Game 7 of the Eastern Conference semifinals.

Duren was a hot topic over the offseason as he and the Pistons struggled to agree on a long-term contract extension. While trade rumors swirled, Detroit got the deal done, signing him to a five-year, $200 million extension that keeps him under contract through the 2030-2031 season.

The Pistons will next face the Los Angeles Lakers at home on Sunday night.

#2852. China wholesale inflation hits highest in more than four years as consumer measure slows

Producer prices in China grew at the fastest rate since June 2022, accelerating for the third month in a row.

The PPI for October came in at 5.7%, compared to the 5.3% expected by economists polled by Reuters. The increase was driven by a sharp rise in prices in the mining and raw materials industries.

The increase was driven by very strong external demand, Zhang Zhiwei, president and chief economist at Pinpoint Asset Management, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia."

Consumer inflation in China slowed to 0.7% in October, its first deceleration after two straight months of increases.

The figure missed expectations of a 0.8% rise from economists polled by Reuters, and was lower than the 0.9% seen in September.

Core inflation, which strips out food and energy prices, came in at 0.8%, down from the 1% seen in the month before.

The rise in consumer prices were still held up by prices of gasoline, which was the largest contributor to the increase in prices, but this was offset by a sharper than expected fall in food prices, according to Dong Lijuan, chief statistician of the urban division at China's National Bureau of Statistics.

The Chinese economy has shown some signs of expansion recently. It grew faster than expected in the third quarter, and September saw a sharp beat in retail sales.

The mainland Chinese CSI 300 index gained 0.56% after the data release.

Pinpoint's Zhang said that domestic demand has been rather weak due to the property market, and this is a key reason why inflation is not picking up in China"There's no clear sign of stabilization [in that sector] yet," he said, adding this was weighing on the household consumer confidence and local government revenue.

"So the the overall economic momentum has been weak and hasn't really seen [a] clear sign of recovery," Zhang explained.

#2848. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

Catellier ranks No. 667 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.60%. See Williams Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2845. Trump says renewed Iran strikes ‘highly unlikely’ if talks progress but warns he could still strike

President Donald Trump said he is "highly unlikely" to restart open war with Iran, but warned he could still strike the country as Washington tries to reach a deal with Tehran to end the conflict.

The now more than eight-month-old war, which stoked inflation and sent oil prices surging, has seen repeated bursts of fighting separated by ceasefires and peace talks for months. It was widely speculated that Trump might restart open conflict after the midterm elections, where his Republican Party lost badly to Democrats.

Trump's comments came during an interview with CNN, where he said restarting the war is unlikely as long as talks to constrain Iran's nuclear program and reopen the Strait of Hormuz make progress.

"It depends on whether or not they sign a very strong agreement," Trump said. "The answer is highly unlikely it's going to be necessary. They're a failing nation."

Trump, however, left the door open for additional strikes if he deems it necessary. The president reiterated his threats to strike bridges and power plants in Iran if the war restarts.

"I've got three aircraft carriers here. The other one just arrived. We have three. They're fully locked and loaded," the president said. "We have 38 bridges in our site that we can knock down in literally one hour."

An end to the war and reopening of the Strait of Hormuz would likely ease oil prices, though finding a truce has proven difficult. All previous attempts to end the war and reopen the strait have been met with disappointment.

Trump is likely to come under increased pressure to end the conflict, however, now that Democrats have won control of both the House and the Senate. Some Republicans are also likely to be eager for an end to the war, given the role high gas prices played in their drubbing at the ballot box.

The president has demanded Iran fully open the Strait of Hormuz and agree to never develop a nuclear weapon as part of any deal.

Later in the day on Truth Social, Trump posted that "Task Force Iron Gate is in full force and effect in the Islamic Republic of Iran."

"Coupled with history's greatest Naval Blockade, very little gets into, or out of, Iran!" he said.

Central Command announced Task Force Iron Gate on Saturday, describing it as an initiative aimed at "preventing the movement of advanced weapons systems, dual-use technologies, sanctioned materials, and illegal revenue streams across Iran's land borders and the Caspian Sea."

The U.S. has maintained a naval blockade of Iranian ports for months.

#2844. From supermarket fridges to passenger flights, a wave of threats is testing Europe's defenses

Cyberattacks, bomb threats against passenger flights, civil unrest and suspected acts of sabotage across Europe have raised questions about the continent's resilience — and how prepared it is to defend itself against modern security threats.

Hackers remotely manipulated refrigeration systems at supermarkets across Europe last week, forcing retailers to discard spoiled food and exposing vulnerabilities in everyday infrastructure.

French blood-bank refrigeration systems were subsequently targeted in what appeared to be a similar incident, while a wave of suspected hoax bomb threats disrupted dozens of flights across the continent, forcing diversions and, in some cases, fighter jet escorts.

The week culminated with bomb threats targeting European news outlets, including the U.K.'s Sky News and Irish public broadcaster RTÉ.

The incidents come against a backdrop of heightened geopolitical tensions and domestic unrest across parts of Europe, with civilian infrastructure potentially becoming an increasingly attractive target for hostile actors.

While authorities have not established who was behind the attacks or whether they were connected, defense and security experts warn that disruption to everyday services can have consequences far beyond the immediate economic damage.

If Russia were responsible, such attacks could be intended to demonstrate the costs of Europe's continued support for Ukraine, said Sidharth Kaushal, a research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute, or RUSI.

"I think it's more about cost imposition," Kaushal told CNBC. "Convincing the European political leadership that the costs of a war can come home to them."

Russia didn't immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

On Monday, supermarkets in Belgium, Luxembourg, Sweden and the Netherlands found that refrigeration systems had been set to as much as 50 degrees Celsius, or 122 degrees Fahrenheit, affecting retailers including Ahold Delhaize, IKEA, Colruyt Group and Carrefour.

It followed an October cyberattack that targeted hundreds of French supermarkets, causing fires, fridges to overheat and food to spoil. The incidents highlighted vulnerabilities in food-supply infrastructure.

They also highlight the role of private companies in national resilience, as internet-connected equipment can be exploited to cause physical disruption.

Fredrik Blix, cybersecurity expert at Visente and associate professor at Stockholm University, told CNBC that the supermarket attacks required very little technical sophistication since vulnerable refrigeration control systems were connected directly to the public internet, allowing potential attackers to find and exploit them.

"These attacks, they don't really require any specific advanced knowledge to carry out," he said, adding that some systems were protected only by default passwords or easily guessed credentials.

"This is why this is so cheap to do," Blix said, warning that the publicity surrounding the incidents could encourage copycat attacks. "Anyone can do it, and it does not require any resources from a country or anything like that."

Cyberattacks have become more common over the past decade as societies have become more digital and vulnerable systems have become easier to access, Blix said. However, he noted that mounting geopolitical tensions have also made European countries more alert to potential hybrid warfare threats, leading to scrutiny by both governments and media.

While Blix acknowledged the possibility of a state or state-backed actor being behind the attacks, he said the more likely scenario is someone testing the boundaries and "having some fun."

Kaushal said the incidents should be viewed within the larger context of suspected sabotage and hybrid threats across Europe, including damage to undersea cables in the Baltic Sea.

"If this was an event in isolation, one might say 'okay, we should view it on its own merits,' but when you view it in the backdrop of all of that, it becomes less clear," he said, also citing death threats against European defense executives and a bomb placed on a DHL aircraft in 2024.

European governments are contending with greater uncertainty over U.S. security guarantees after decades of underinvestment in defense, while facing a rise in unconventional but potentially devastating threats.

Hybrid warfare combines tactics such as cyberattacks, sabotage and disinformation to destabilize adversaries without triggering open military conflict.

Countries' abilities to withstand hybrid threats vary substantially across the continent, according to Kaushal. While Finland and Sweden have well-developed civilian defense programs reflecting their Cold War experience, much of Europe's infrastructure was built around economic efficiency rather than defense needs.

"As one goes further west in Europe, the level of preparation, both against hybrid attacks, but also just simple missile attacks of the kind that we've seen in Ukraine, is far less great," he said.

"Every attack generates a bit of resilience," he said, citing NATO's increased focus on threats to underwater cables and gas pipelines after the 2022 Nord Stream sabotage.

Oana Popescu-Zamfir, a senior research fellow specializing in European security at RUSI, said the recent incidents showed Europe had lagged in "preparing for something that we have known would be coming."

"We've been slow in adjusting to a new reality," she said. "We still tend to conceptually divide between peace and wartime, and that is no longer the case because we're not at war, but we're also not in peace time."

She said Europe was likely to face sustained hybrid attacks designed to achieve military objectives without conventional military force.

Popescu-Zamfir said Russia could draw on networks of proxies in Europe, some directly controlled and others more loosely coordinated, making responsibility difficult to trace directly to the Kremlin.

She told CNBC that "the damage that can be done is huge" in targeted attacks on internet-connected systems, citing the potential disruption of blood reserves.

The attacks are not a "piece of cake for Russia to carry out, but they're also not rocket science," she said, adding that they "can do tremendous damage in concrete terms and in terms of the psychological impact on our societies and the feeling that we are unprotected."

Blix said companies can take relatively straightforward steps to reduce their exposure.

"These are basic cyber hygiene things... You should not be allowed to connect industrial control systems and this kind of operational technology directly online without any proper security around it," he said.

Responsibility is split among product manufacturers, maintenance providers and grocery chains, making accountability unclear as each points to the others.

Blix said maintenance companies sometimes prioritize convenience by enabling remote access despite manufacturers' security guidance, while retailers may fail to enforce adequate cybersecurity standards among suppliers.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," Blix said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

Europe has also seen widespread unrest in recent months, adding political pressure as households grapple with high living costs.

Domestic political and economic tensions can also give foreign adversaries opportunities to deepen divisions, said Alexander Anderson, president and director of policy at the Centre for International Security and Economic Strategy.

He told CNBC that Russian and potentially Chinese influence operations may work to amplify those divisions.

"But we should be careful not to dismiss legitimate domestic grievances as the product of foreign interference," he said. "Doing so risks misunderstanding both the causes of political instability and the methods through which hostile actors seek to exploit it."

Weeks of nationwide protests, riots and strikes have increased pressure on the French government, complicating efforts to rein in public finances and deepening existing political divisions.

In Spain, demonstrations and a parliamentary impasse over the housing crisis led Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez to call a snap election. The vote is scheduled for Nov. 29. The far-right Vox party is expected to make gains, putting Sanchez's left-leaning government at risk of losing power.

Germany, Italy, Portugal and Albania have also seen unrest over rising living costs in recent weeks.

On Friday, protesters in Amsterdam tried to enter a Microsoft data center, Reuters reported.

Meanwhile, elevated energy prices, persistent inflation, sluggish growth and large budget deficits are pushing up borrowing costs and leaving governments with less room to support struggling households.

#2840. Nvidia investigates CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with alleged Chinese spy

Nvidia has opened an investigation into co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang's relationship with a businesswoman who intelligence agencies say is a Chinese spy.

U.S. intelligence agencies have expressed concerns that the woman, Alice Huang, who is affiliated with a cloud company and an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed, has been passing information to Beijing, according to reports in The New York Times and the Financial Times.

Huang told Nvidia staff in an email on Sunday that he had an affair with the woman and that Nvidia's board of directors had opened an independent investigation into the spying allegations.

"I was unfaithful to my wife. My actions hurt her and our children. There is no excuse for what I did," Huang wrote in the message. "I am deeply sorry."

An Nvidia spokesperson said that Huang was committed to continuing as CEO of Nvidia without interruption.

In a statement, the Nvidia board said it would investigate the allegations that Huang had been the target of a foreign intelligence effort.

"While the Board has no reason to believe that either national security or NVIDIA secrets were compromised, the Board's inquiry will examine all available information," Nvidia's board said in a statement.

Read Huang's email to Nvidia employees and the company board of directors' statement here.

The allegations come as Nvidia has become the center of a trade dispute between China and the U.S. as AI is seen as increasingly critical to national security.

The U.S. restricts exports of Nvidia's most advanced AI chips to China, though it has approved exports of certain older, slower models.

Megaspeed, which Ms. Huang is affiliated with, has been accused of helping restricted Chinese companies acquire Nvidia AI chips. Nvidia representatives previously said that Megaspeed had already been investigated for evading export controls.

Nvidia's Huang has pushed for broader AI chip exports to China, saying it is better for the U.S. if the world uses and builds on American technology. He has also aligned with President Donald Trump on American artificial intelligence policy and its infrastructure buildout.

In his note to staff, Huang said he was "proud and grateful" to be an American.

Three House Republican committee chairs sent a letter to Huang on Wednesday asking him to sit for a closed-door briefing with lawmakers on Nov. 18.

Nvidia reports third-quarter earnings on Nov. 17 and will hold a major conference in Washington, D.C., at the end of the month.

#2836. Steve Sarkisian lauds the undeniable toughness of his Texas team

Casting the entire football world as doubting the ability of the No. 7 Texas Longhorns to go into Columbia and beat the No. 18 Missouri Tigers is a real stretch, but it was much more believable when head coach Steve Sarkisian lauded the undeniable toughness of his football team after securing a fourth ranked win this season as the Longhorns navigate a nine-game SEC schedule for the first time.

"The DNA of this organization is mental and physical toughness and I think that poured out of our pores today in this ball game," Sarkisian said.

Now through the fifth game in the eight-game stretch without a bye week to end the regular season, Texas is banged up, playing without three key starters and receiving just 18 snaps from star edge Colin Simmons in his return from a high ankle sprain.

"If you'd have told me we walk in here without Trevor Goosby, without Cam Coleman, and a very limited Colin Simmons, with some other banged up receivers — by the way, those other three are probably going to go in the top 15 picks of the draft — I don't know how many other teams could do that," Sarkisian said.

Texas did it largely by relying on the depth accumulated through years of highly-ranked recruiting classes, as well as key transfer portal additions stepping forward, like running back Raleek Brown, who caught the go-ahead touchdown pass on a wheel route and put the game away with his eight-yard rushing touchdown with 1:25 remaining.

Of the 101 rushing yards gained by the three Texas running backs who played, Brown notched 86 yards on 17 carries. And while he didn't break any runs longer than 13 yards, that was a credit to some solid situational tackling by Missouri more than the regular busts in execution and assignment from Kyle Flood's offensive line, which somewhat steadied itself on Saturday.

"One thing about Raleek, he might not be the biggest guy in stature, but he's got a huge heart," Sarkisian said. "This guy's a very prideful player. He plays hard. He provides a ton of energy, not just to the offense, but the entire team."

At wide receiver, Emmett Mosley V led the Longhorns with 85 receiving yards on five catches. Missouri played more zone defense than expected, and Mosley kept the Texas offense moving by regularly finding holes in it, affording quarterback Arch Manning the type of intermediate throws that tend not to suffer from his inconsistencies.

Next to Mosley, who played every snap, St. Louis product Ryan Wingo was a warrior next to him for 71 all plays even though he practiced minimally on Thursday before participating in the Friday walkthrough. Wingo notched six catches for 52 yards and one carry for six yards.

"I'm really happy for Ryan — I know Ryan wanted this game and he wanted to play in the game. Like I said, he was not 100 percent, but he made some really critical plays for us today," Sarkisian said.

"We've got to get him healthier again for next week. So happy for him, happy for his family. I know this game meant a lot to him," Sarkisian said.

Defensively, more players had to emerge with the prevalence of injuries.

Because Simmons was limited, other edge players had to step up, including senior Colton Vasek, who battled shoulder injuries throughout his career. Over the last three games, Vasek has played half of his season total of snaps, recording four tackles (two solo) and coming up with a big sack in his 18 snaps against the Tigers.

While nickel back Graceson Littleton was able to battle through 51 snaps after he was listed as questionable on the early injury reports, cornerback Kade Phillips played 33 snaps, showing some toughness of his own — Phillips was targeted three times in coverage, but didn't give up a reception.

Defensive coordinator Will Muschamp leaned on cornerback Warren Roberson to handle the snaps that Littleton and Phillips missed. As Roberson received his first career start, Missouri predictably targeted the inconsistent senior regularly, but beyond a pass-interference penalty on the first drive, Roberson mostly held up well.

Tigers quarterback Austin Simmons targeted Roberson on 21 percent of his pass attempts, including nearly a quarter of the throws that Pro Football Focus tracked as targeting a specific player in coverage. Normally an efficient passing offense, Missouri completed three passes for 35 yards on the nine throws with Roberson in coverage, 3.9 yards per attempt.

By itself, Roberson's coverage success made it his best performance at Texas, ultimately magnified by his crunching hit on Cayden Lee that caused the fumble returned by Littleton to the goal line that set up Brown's game-sealing touchdown run.

"Guys have to step up — that's the nature of the beast, but like I told the team last night, we don't recruit really well around here for fun. It's not for Signing Day. It's for moments like this that we can have quality people that we trust that can go in and play," Sarkisian said.

The Texas head coach made changes to his culture-building approach this offseason, starting Culture Wednesdays during the spring instead of the summer to reflect the reality that the vast majority of incoming players now arrive in January.

Now that's paying off, in Sarkisian's estimation.

"This team gets tighter — the more adversity we get, the tighter we get. I think it's a cultural thing, I think it's a toughness thing, and I think it's a leadership thing. I'm proud of our coaches. I'm proud of the the leaders on this team to galvanize everybody three weeks ago or so to continue to fight back," Sarkisian said.

After the home loss to Florida marked back-to-back defeats against unranked opponents, the Longhorns were reeling, with questions about Sarkisian's role as the offensive play caller, the role of Jeff Banks as the special teams coordinator, and the role of starting quarterback Arch Manning, embattled after game-changing turnovers.

Manning in particular has raised his level of play, going two games without throwing an interception and operating without the hesitancy that characterized his indecisive performance against Florida.

And Sarkisian has made some adjustments to the offense by relying less on shot plays, going back to the running back screen game, and generally calling more quick passes to get the ball out of Manning's hand before the protection breaks down.

Right now, with one of the best resumes in the country, the Longhorns are survivors. Challenges remain, starting with next week's trip to Death Valley to face the Tigers, but over the last three games, Texas has found ways to win again.

"We knew it was going to be a long season. The season wasn't over. It was going to be a long season, and it's not over today," Sarkisian said. "We've got more games to go play. We've got another tough one next week, but we're going to surely enjoy this one for the for for next eight 16 or so hours."

#2824. Blackhawks Sticking With Blashill & Staff Despite Disastrous Start

Chicago Blackhawks general manager Kyle Davidson is scheduled to speak with the media Wednesday afternoon, but isn't expected to discuss any changes involving Jeff Blashill and the coaching staff.

According to team sources, Blashill will remain the Blackhawks head coach despite a disastrous 4-13-1 start to the 2026-27 regular season, with the club currently riding a six-game losing streak. Assistant coaches Anders Sorensen, Michael Peca, and Mike Vellucci are also safe for now.

BREAKING: The Blackhawks will not make a coaching change and Jeff Blashill will remain their head coach ⤵️ https://t.co/JQokCEmojF

— Scott Powers (@ByScottPowers) November 8, 2026

The Blackhawks have fallen remarkably short of the preseason expectations through 18 games, with only four wins and the NHL's second-worst points percentage. With hopes of at least competing for a Stanley Cup Playoff spot in Year 5 of Davidson's rebuild, being ranked towards the bottom of the league in every major statistical category is beyond disappointing.

Goals scored per game? They're 31st among 32 teams. Goals against per game? 31st. Shots on goal per game? 31st. 5v5 shot attempts per game? Dead-last.

And it's not just the numbers suggesting the Blackhawks have been downright terrible. The eye test backs it up plenty, with TNT analyst Paul Bissonnette hitting the nail on the head last Wednesday while breaking down the tape against the Florida Panthers.

Bissonnette blasted the Blackhawks on national television, calling them a "soft" team and describing their timid brand of hockey as "Mickey Mouse" and "AHL-level". The former NHL enforcer told no lies, with the standard set far too low for Chicago.

Then, just four nights later, the Blackhawks were embarrassed once again, getting pounded 9-2 by the defending champion Carolina Hurricanes on Saturday night en route to a sixth straight loss. It also marked the team's ninth consecutive road loss to start the season, with a 0-8-1 record.

The debacle in Carolina capped off a 0-3-0 Southeast road trip, with the Hawks getting outscored 18-6. Now back in Chicago with four days off before returning to action, the timing seemed right for Davidson and/or chairman Danny Wirtz to make a significant change, but it hasn't happened yet.

Ultimately, the Blackhawks elected not to go that route with Blashill right now, although it seems inevitable eventually, barring a drastic turnaround.

Instead, the coaching staff and management reportedly got together on Sunday to discuss potential adjustments and changes moving forward. Some of those changes could become clearer when the team returns to the ice for practice on Monday morning.

One has already been revealed, as backup goaltender Arvid Soderblom was placed on waivers Sunday. The Blackhawks later recalled Drew Commesso from the Rockford IceHogs, signaling a new No. 2 netminder behind Spencer Knight.

#2819. 49ers rookie defensive tackle Gracen Halton suffers season-ending broken ankle in win over Raiders

The San Francisco 49ers maintained their place atop the NFL standings with Sunday's 38-17 win over the Las Vegas Raiders, improving their record to 7-1.

However, a team that's already been hit hard by injuries lost one of its best young defensive players in the Week 9 win. Rookie defensive tackle Gracen Halton broke his left ankle and will miss the remainder of the season, head coach Kyle Shanahan announced.

Halton, 22, suffered the injury during the fourth quarter when fellow defensive lineman Keion White was blocked by Raiders tackle DJ Glaze and fell onto his lower leg. Medical personnel applied an air cast to Helton's left leg and he had to be carted off the field.

In eight games (six starts), Halton led the 49ers with five tackles for loss and was second on the team with two sacks. He recorded 15 total tackles with a forced fumble.

"He's played at a very high level as a rookie," Shanahan said after the game. "He's had an awesome seven or eight games here. We're gonna miss him a lot."

Halton was San Francisco's fourth-round pick in the 2026 NFL Draft (No. 107 overall) out of Oklahoma.

"I'm sick for him," linebacker Fred Warner told NBC Sports Bay Area's Bonta Hill. "I know he was emotional getting on that cart, but I let him know, God, He does everything for a reason. So just keep his head up and he'll be all right."

The 49ers acquired defensive tackle DeForest Buckner from the Indianapolis Colts last week, which bumped Halton from the starting lineup. But the depth on the interior defensive line and ability to rotate players in and out is now obviously affected by Halton's season-ending injury.

Cornerbacks Deommodore Lenoir (foot) and Renardo Green (back) also left Sunday's game with injuries and did not return. Shanahan said both players would be re-evaluated on Monday and haven't been ruled out for Week 11's matchup with the Dallas Cowboys.

#2816. Sri Lanka captain Mendis wins toss, sends a new-look Pakistan lineup in to bat in 1st cricket test

RAWALPINDI, Pakistan (AP) — Sri Lanka captain Kusal Mendis won the toss and elected to bowl first Monday in the first cricket test against a new-look Pakistan lineup containing four players on debut.

Arafat Minhas, a spinning allrounder and player of the recent triangular ODI series, fast bowler Saqib Khan and top-order batters Tayyab Arif and Mohammad Taha were all on debut for the hosts.

Sri Lanka is ranked No. 7 in the WTC standings, winning just one of its six test matches while drawing three and losing two.

Mendis, who replaced Dhananjaya de Silva as test skipper in October, hoped his trio of fast bowlers will utilize the tinge of grass left on the wicket against an inexperienced Pakistan top-order as the tourists left out their front-line spinner Prabath Jayasuriya.

Sri Lanka has a recent happy memory at the Rawalpindi Cricket Stadium where it achieved the second-highest successful run chase in ODI history — 430 against England in the tri-series last month.

#2809. Patriots DB Christian Gonzalez Reacts to Performance vs. Packers

There were very few New England Patriots players who had good games on Sunday against the Green Bay Packers. However, arguably the worst performance on the field came from Pro Bowl cornerback Christian Gonzalez.

PFF initially charted out the game for Gonzalez. According to them, he was targeted 11 times on 36 coverage snaps. On those 11 targets, he allowed 10 receptions. That was for 187 yards and 40 yards after the catch. He would allow a touchdown and only have one pass breakup.

By no means was Gonzalez the sole reason why the Patriots lost 45-27. However, when the player you paid $135 million, making him the highest-paid player at his position, performs like that, it's almost impossible to overcome mistakes by other players.

Following the game, Patriots DB Christian Gonzalez knew how poorly he performed. There was never any avoiding it, and he admitted how poorly he played in the loss.

"They got the best of me today … I had a bad game," Gonzalez said. "I'm not shaken up by it. I'm not too worried about it. I'll move on and be ready next week."

Patriots head coach Mike Vrabel wasn't afraid to call out Gonzalez either. He publicly shared what he thought of the performance, and he also made it clear that it's hard to expect the team to win without one of its best players performing to their standard.

"When your best players don't play good, you don't have much of a chance in this league," Vrabel said.

Gonzalez previously missed time this season with a shoulder injury that he suffered back in Week 3. That cost him a couple of games.

"I mean, they did a good job," Gonzalez said. "He came out and ran good routes. Ran the routes they had in the gameplan and Jordan Love was able to get him the ball. I just gotta be able to finish and make those plays. I'm right there, I just gotta make those plays. I mean, it's not the end of the world. I had a bad game. I'm not shaken up by it, I'm not too worried about it. I'll move on and be ready for next week."

It doesn't take a football genius to see that the gameplan didn't work on defense for the Patriots. 45 points is the most the Patriots have allowed in a regular season game in 33 years. It's also the most points they've allowed at home since 1970.

Gonzalez addressed that gameplan after the game. However, as he explained, things went wrong when he couldn't take away the X for the Packers.

"The plan to stop the X plays I mean was just — just, like, playing our defense," Gonzalez said. "There's things that go into it… I can't fall on a go route. I can't turn and get tripped up. The defense is counting on me to hold down the backside, hold down everything else just like we have been. I mean, just stopping it. I mean, that was just the plan. Obviously, we didn't and they did a better job."

The Patriots and Gonzalez will need to bounce back quickly from the loss. Now 5-4 on the season, they'll head to Europe to take on a tough Detroit Lions team.

#2808. Charles Bassey’s play is turning heads in Golden State

Every season I activate this trope to try and inspire a wary Dub Nation who wonders why we don't replace (insert suspect player) with someone else in the league. You're in the back seat of your mom's car, those sweet sweet Golden Arches come into view, and you make your pitch: "Mom, can we PLEAAASEEE get McDonald's?"

And without missing a beat, back comes the dreamcrushing sentence masquerading as a better suggestion: "We got McDonald's at home."

What's at home is never McDonald's, but it's ground beef, sandwich bread, ketchup, mustard, and relish. You eat it and more often than not depending on who your mama is, it hits. Your tastebuds are awash with flavor and gratitude. Maybe mom was right, maybe you do have McDonalds at home.

That's Charles Bassey for the Warriors right now. When Golden State asked for a real center, the front office looked at the cap sheet and injury report, and said, "we got a center at home."

And maybe the Warriors front office is right, maybe we DO have a big man at home!

And that is quite the development considering the hope of Kristaps Porziņģis dominating as the big man for the Warriors has effectively died as it looks like he's retiring from Warriors basketball. The team re-signed him this summer even though he'd missed 90 games over the past two seasons with three different teams.

Then there's Al Horford, who is 40 years old and dealing with a body banged up from years of playoff warfare. Horford is a professional basketball genius. But asking him to play 30 minutes a night in November is not gonna have him available for any kinda playoff run in the spring.

And of course the Warriors can always counter with the grizzled, undersized legend Draymond Green at center. That works until Draymond does Draymond things and gets ejected two minutes into the third quarter, which brings us to the night the center at home showed up.

On Nov. 2 against the Suns, Golden State trailed by 17. Draymond had been thrown out for body slamming one Phoenix player and quasi strangling Dillon Brooks (rightfully so).

That's when Bassey had a career night, scoring 19 points on 7-of-10 shooting, grabbed 8 rebounds and finished +10. After Draymond's ejection, the Warriors outscored Phoenix 59-45. He also built a connection with rookie Yaxel Lendeborg. Four of Lendeborg's five assists that night went to Bassey. Every time the rookie lofted the ball near the rim, Bassey caught it and finished.

After the game, Bassey called out the Suns for "fake toughness." The center at home has a little seasoning on him, don't he?

This wasn't a one-night fluke. The Warriors started 0-3, then won seven straight to get to 7-3, and Bassey was in the middle of it:

He has a +34 plus-minus over the seven-game streak, and +33 in the five games where he played real minutes

His 15.7 rebounds per 36 minutes ranks second in the NBA

He's shooting 63.6% from the field and 88.2% from the line, with a 25.8 PER.

Over the streak he's averaging roughly 21 points, 15 rebounds and nearly 3 blocks per 36 minutes.

Ummm ladies and gentlemen, he's really balling in his role right now. Here's the case for more Bassey minutes: in the Orlando game, he played only 11. The Magic won the offensive rebounding battle 24-3 and outscored Golden State 33-2 on second-chance points. The Warriors still won in overtime, because Steph Curry is a supernatural force of nature on the basketball court.

Bassey isn't Porziņģis, a guy who stretches the floor to 28 feet or make anyone's All-Star ballot. He's 6-foot-9 and focused completely on rebounding, rim-running and blocked shots. He's also available to play, and we all know the best ability is availability.

Warriors GM Mike Dunleavy may still trade for a center; I absolutely think he should. Using Porziņģis' contract to match salaries for a real big makes sense, and they have a real chance to pull it off before the trade deadline.

Until then, Dub Nation asked for McDonald's and got told they had a center at home, and so far the one at home is providing a lot of sustenance.

Colts quarterback Daniel Jones has heard the trade speculation. How could he not?

After the Colts traded Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner, other teams are likely to make inquiries about the availability of Jones, running back Jonathan Taylor and wide receiver Josh Downs, if they haven't already. Stephen Holder of ESPN reports that the Colts have already received calls about Jones.

Jones called the swirling rumors unhelpful after the 27-23 loss to the Cowboys on Sunday, but added the team needs to do a better job handling things out of their control.

"There's been plenty of distractions the last couple weeks with all the stuff that's gone on, and it's unfortunate we're in that position," Jones said. "But I think as players, your job is to focus as much on what's in front of you. And playing quarterback, there's a lot focusing on getting ready for a game. So, that was my approach."

Jones, who was 20-of-34 for 180 yards with a touchdown and an interception in Sunday's loss, was asked whether he expects to remain in Indianapolis after Tuesday's trade deadline. He talked around the question.

Jones is likely to remain with the Colts for the rest of this season, Holder reports.

Felix Nmecha put in a hero's shift as Borussia Dortmund sealed a 2-1 win over SV Elversberg this afternoon. The central midfielder struck twice after the break to ensure victory for the hosts who stay level with Bundesliga leaders Bayern Munich into the international break.

That set up a grand slam ending, with the gutsy Saarland guests surging forward with hope. Substitute forward Adam Raif was next to test Kobel. A flurry of Keidel set-pieces in injury time came close, but Niko Kovač's side hung on to the maximum reward for a 2-1 triumph.

#2804. Is Luka Dončić playing tonight? Injury update on Lakers star vs. Pistons

The Los Angeles Lakers are tasked with facing one of the NBA's best defenses in the Detroit Pistons in the first of their four-game road trip on Sunday, Nov. 8. They'll have the added challenge of doing so without the driving force of their offense.

Luka Dončić has been ruled out with lower back tightness, the team announced. Dončić was listed as questionable on Saturday, one day after the leading MVP candidate dropped a season-high 53 points at home against the Portland Trail Blazers.

It's a blow to the Lakers (8-2), who will be without Dončić's 40.1 points, 7.8 rebounds and 7.7 assists per night in nine games played this season. He's missed one other game with back tightness.

Detroit (6-2), meanwhile, has given up an average of 101.8 points per game, fewest in the league.

Amid Dončić's historic stretch of seven consecutive 35-plus point games, the last four of which he's scored at least 40, head coach JJ Redick was asked if there was any danger in relying too much on the two-time scoring champion.

"Part of planning and team building and playing an 82-game season is that you're constantly assessing and trying to be better," Redick said on Friday. "... Let's say you're running a nine-man rotation or 10-man rotation. It's rare that you have each guy playing great. The goal is for each guy to play great, but that just doesn't happen so you're constantly tending to things. It's just the NBA.

" ... We have to rely on Luka because he's our best player, just like every other team relies on their best player. We still have to coach everyone else and coach the team."

The Lakers could be choosing to be proactive with getting rest for Dončić, as they travel to Atlanta for the second night of a back-to-back on Monday.

Dončić does have a history of back injuries during his career. Before this season, he was most recently listed with a back injury in March 2025. He was also listed with back-related injuries during the 2019, 2021 and 2024 seasons.

Dončić entered his third season with the Lakers as the unquestioned leader. He was ranked No. 4 on USA TODAY Sports' top 100 player rankings ahead of the 2026-27 season and is currently No. 1 on Lorenzo Reyes' MVP power rankings.

#2801. In Broncos-Panthers, replay was used to determine whether replay should be used

In Sunday's Broncos-Panthers game, Denver coach Sean Payton activated the replay process at a key moment, turning an incompletion into an interception.

As it turns out, the NFL also used replay to determine whether replay should be used to give the Broncos possession after Panthers tight end poppe the ball into the air in the end zone and Broncos safety Devon Key scooped it up before it touched the ground..

In a pool report, NFL V.P. of instant replay Mark Butterworth explained that replays were consulted to determine whether Payton had thrown the red challenge flag before the next snap.

"Replay can get involved to make sure that the head coach throws his challenge flag prior to the next snap," Butterworth said. "We were actually able to use angles to show that the flag was out prior to the ball being snapped, and so we permitted the challenge."

Although the rulebook doesn't expressly state that replay may be used to determine whether replay can be used, the NFL relies on the first sentence of Rule 15, Section 9: "The Replay Official or designated members of the Officiating department may provide information regarding the correct application of the playing rules."

And while Section 9 lists several specific examples as to when such consultation is permitted, the list is preceded by the phrase "including, but not limited to." Which means that there may be other situations in which the Replay Official and/or the league office may assist the on-field officials.

One of those situations is whether a coach properly activated the replay process. Which allows replay to be used to determine whether replay should be used.

#2797. 32 things we learned in NFL Week 9: QB crises testing teams as backups flounder

The 32 things we learned from Week 9 of the 2026 NFL season:

0. Still, the number of wins by the Los Angeles Chargers, who lost Sunday to the Houston Texans and dropped to 0-8 for the first time since 2000 when – you might have read this before – Jim Harbaugh was one of their quarterbacks … though it was the Ryan Leaf era then. This year should be more painful for Harbaugh, given both his coaching résumé and the talent on this roster – and the Bolts are apparently unload to part with much, if any, of it even as Tuesday's 4 p.m. ET trade deadline draws ever nearer.

1. The jersey number of speedy Texans WR Tank Dell, who scored his first TD in nearly two years – a 63-yard reception from C.J. Stroud – after a gruesome knee injury, which included multiple ligament tears, near the end of the 2024 season derailed his career.

2. The number of punters who completed a pass in Sunday's game between the Detroit Lions and Miami Dolphins, both the Lions' Jack Fox and Dolphins' Bradley Pinion converting first downs.

3. The number of sacks by Los Angeles Rams OLB Myles Garrett in his first action with his new team since he failed to even record a tackle in his Week 1 debut in Australia while he was battling a knee injury. But while LA (5-4) peeked above .500 for the first time this season, Rams fans can only hope WR Puka Nacua's new knee issue doesn't keep him down for long.

3a. Also the number of sacks by New York Giants rookie LB Arvell Reese – something of a coming-out party given he had 1½ in his first seven games – in his team's 19-11 defeat of the Philadelphia Eagles.

4. The number of times the Lions went for it – and converted – on fourth down in Sunday's 19-9 defeat of the Dolphins.

5. More importantly, Detroit fulfilled its near-term mission of notching a W for injured LB Jack Campbell, who remains hospitalized after suffering a scary neck injury in Week 8. Campbell, who is reportedly determined to resume his career, remains at the forefront of his teammates' minds.

"A lot of what we said was win No. 6 for 46," Lions coach Dan Campbell told reporters on Sunday. "And we did that today. We got that done."

6. The number of quarterbacking changes, necessitated by various reasons, in Week 9. Sunday saw the Cleveland Browns' Shedeur Sanders, Philadelphia's Joe Milton and the Arizona Cardinals' Garnder Minshew II all make their first starts of the 2026 – none of them successful. Minshew's Cards' were swept by the Seattle Seahawks and their QB2, Drew Lock, who stood in for injured Sam Darnold and vanquished Arizona for the second time this season – though an oblique injury raises the possibility Lock could join Darnold on the sideline in Week 10. The Baltimore Ravens' Joe Fagnano lost his first career start Thursday night against the Jacksonville Jaguars.

7. The number of times New York Jets WR Adonai Mitchell was targeted in Sunday's 35-12 loss to the Kansas City Chiefs. He didn't catch a single pass.

8. The number of times Chargers QB Justin Herbert was sacked by the Texans on Sunday, the most abuse a Bolts passer has absorbed in a half-century.

9. To illustrate how much the QB carousel has spun in 2026, Jacoby Brissett, who just lost his starting job to Minshew in Arizona, is expected to replace Fagnano by becoming the Ravens' fourth different staring quarterback this season on "Monday Night Football" in Week 10. Brissett was officially obtained from the Cardinals on Friday and would face the winless Chargers if that is indeed his Baltimore debut.

10. Speaking of QB2s on Monday night, the Minnesota Vikings are turning to Carson Wentz this week against the Buffalo Bills as Kyler Murray rehabs a sore hamstring … though it's worth wondering if either he or Wentz will be the Vikes' starter on the other side of the trade deadline.

11.  New Orleans Saints WR Jordyn Tyson caught four passes for 30 yards – and also lost a fumble – in his NFL debut Sunday. A hamstring injury suffered in training camp delayed the start of his career.

12. With Tyson now in action, Los Angeles Rams QB Ty Simpson is the only first-rounder from the 2026 draft who has yet to play.

13. The number of penalties (for 119 yards) committed by the Carolina Panthers on Sunday. They lost to the Denver Broncos.

14. The number of penalties (for 153 yards) committed by the Dallas Cowboys on Sunday. They beat the Indianapolis Colts.

15. The play of Week 9? How about Broncos DB Devon Key's amazing, heads-up interception in Charlotte which completely changed the momentum of the game Denver eventually won.

16. The number of consecutive regular-season games decided by one score which the Broncos have now won – a new NFL record.

17. Sanders had perhaps the best game of his career – his 114.6 passer rating was a personal best for the Browns' second-year passer – but he came an overtime two-point conversion shy of upending the Saints in the Superdome. TBD what Sanders' 297-yard day (with two TD passes) means for incumbent QB1 Deshaun Watson, who sat out Sunday after clearing concussion protocol late in the week.

18. The number of touches (for 64 yards) Colts RB Jonathan Taylor had in Sunday's loss to Dallas, potentially his final game with Indianapolis pending what happens ahead of the trade deadline – Taylor perhaps the most prized player who remains potentially available. He had averaged nearly 23 touches over the first six games, but it's perhaps not surprising the Colts have reduced his workload lately.

19. How can a team as talented as the Cowboys nearly find a way to lose to a team as decimated as the Colts? Dallas outgained Indianapolis 508-271, averaged 2.6 yards more per play and dominated time of possession, holding the ball for nearly 11 more minutes of game time. But when you draw that many yellow flags, turn the ball over twice and even your normally elite kicker is missing field goals, you remind everyone why you might not be good enough to win even the downtrodden NFC East.

20. What a wild day for the Eagles' Braden Mann, half of his eight punts covering 60 yards – one at the end of the first half bombed 86 yards, the longest in the league in a quarter-century.

21. Yet Mann wound up with an unremarkable 48-yard gross average on the day given another punt traveled 17 yards, while his worst went for minus-2 yards. Woof.

22. Here's hoping the Chiefs don't have to dig down their QB depth chart after Patrick Mahomes experienced what he described as "scary" elbow injury in Sunday's shellacking of the Jets. Mahomes didn't miss a play but will have more testing done this week.

23. Justin Fields is Mahomes' backup nowadays … in case you were wondering.

24. In the meantime, Mahomes and Travis Kelce did break the record for most passing yards by a quarterback-tight end duo in NFL history, surpassing the mark of former AFC West rivals Philip Rivers and Antonio Gates.

25. Defensively, Chiefs DT Chris Jones had two sacks Sunday, the three-time All-Pro's first full sacks of the season. He entered Sunday with a half-sack through the season's first two months.

26. Who needs a running game when your top two receivers are doing things the storied Green Bay Packers have never accomplished in the Super Bowl era (since 1966)?

27. And while the Pack whacked the New England Patriots 45-27 in front of Pats legends Tom Brady and Rob Gronkowski, shoutout to Gronk on the weekend he entered the team's Hall of Fame.

28. Props to Las Vegas Raiders TE Brock Bowers, who now has 224 career catches – the most ever by a tight end in his first three NFL seasons. And Bowers obviously has a chance to now blow well past Trey McBride's previous mark (221).

29. Props to the Cincinnati Bengals, whose defeat of the Atlanta Falcons on Sunday in Madrid tied them with the idle Pittsburgh Steelers for the AFC North lead. More noteworthy, the Stripes' TD celebrations in front of Spanish fans in the iconic home (Bernabéu) of Real Madrid were most definitely on point.

30. Brother on brother crime overseas, Falcons DB Sydney Brown tackling his identical twin brother, Bengals RB Chase Brown, for the first time in the NFL.

31. Brother on brother crime in the heartland, Cowboys DB Caleb Downs trash talking older brother Josh Downs, a wideout for the Colts, when he dropped a pass … and nearly got literally lit up by the rookie. Adding insult to insult, Caleb Downs intercepted Indy's final pass of the day to put the four-point win on ice for Dallas.

32. Congratulations to the real vets, not the NFL variety, who will be celebrated Wednesday on Veterans Day. And a shoutout to the NFL, which has raised more than $80 million for its military nonprofit partners since 2011, when the league instituted its "Salute to Service" program, which will be in high gear from Weeks 9 through 11.
