#2758. 9 winners, 2 losers, and 1 IDK from the 49ers win over the Raiders

The San Francisco 49ers made it interesting after a wild third-quarter sequence that saw multiple turnovers in 10 minutes, but the offense was too much for the Raiders.

Here are the winners and losers from the Niners' high-scoring affair.

Watkins caught a 37-yard post from Brock Purdy for a touchdown. He caught all four of his targets for 86 yards. If Watkins can be reliable like he was in Week 9, it'll make the loss of Mike Evans more tolerable if he's out for multiple games. The Niners really don't have that big-play threat. On Sunday, it was Watkins.

Head coach Kyle Shanahan said Watkins took a "huge step forward" on Sunday.

Black led the Niners in rushing with 70 yards. He had a couple of double-digit scampers. Black had 52 yards after contact. He was a beast on outside zone, with 40 yards on five carries. Black played fast, looked fast, and allowed the Niners to take some off the plate of Christian McCaffrey.

Shanahan said, "I thought KB ran real hard today, and was real happy with him. He has been improving. Hope he keeps doing that."

Greenlaw made a great diving play to recover Fred Warner's forced fumble. It wasn't an easy play, and didn't look like he'd stay in bounds.

Greenlaw led the team with eight tackles — three for loss — and had five impact plays. Greenlaw was excellent at limiting damage in coverage. He was targeted five times, gave up four receptions, but for -5 yards.

Osa Odighizuwa had a pair of tackles for loss and quarterback hits. It was arguably his most impactful game of the season. He finished with a 17.6% pressure rate. Sumer Sports had Odighizuwa down for four impact plays, tying for second on the defense.

What a weapon it is when you have a quarterback who can bail you out of any situation, thanks to his legs. That was the case with Purdy on Sunday. Purdy ran for 40 yards on seven attempts. He had a couple of key first-down carries, including one for 18 yards. The 49ers ran for 190 yards, and their quarterback was a big reason why.

Shanahan said, "He was effective with it," about Purdy's scrambling. "There were times when he missed a couple, where he probably didn't wanna scramble. But once he starts that, you're upset until he moves the chains or scores a touchdown, and then you get over it pretty quick. Brock has been a threat all year with his legs, and continues to do it."

Shanahan added later that Purdy made up for some of the missed throws earlier in the game with plays later in the game.

Kirk Cousins threw the ball directly to Jaden Dugger for a pick-six. But he didn't see Renardo Green in the end zone and underthrew another deep pass that fell into Malik Mustapha's lap.

If he continues this pace, the peanut punch will be named after Fred Warner.

A bit of luck was involved on the fumble right at the goal line. That is the 49ers defense. Bend-but-don't-break. They keep everything in front of you, prey on mistakes, and that's what happened against the Raiders.

The 49ers had zero answers for Bowers, who caught seven of his eight targets for 111 yards, and had a receiving EPA of +0.72 every time the ball was in his hands. It's one thing to give up a big play to a superstar player, but Bowers had two receptions for 50 yards on crossing routes, caught a 21-yarder on a post route, and a 20-yarder on a dig. It was big play after big play.

If somebody else had beaten the Niners, that would have been respectable. But the other team's best player got over on the defense, which is concerning.

The 49ers were uncharacteristically sloppy, especially early on offense. The offense averages three penalties a game, and topped that by the second quarter. For the game, the 49ers had seven penalties for 59 yards. Vegas only benefited from those with one first down, but if you give a playoff team that many free yards, they'll take advantage.

Purdy overcame both penalties with his legs on one drive. While he's playing at a high level, it's not realistic to expect your quarterback to bail you out of those situations.

Kirk Cousins was only pressured 31.3% of the time, which was 24th so far heading into Sunday Night Football in Week 9 among all quarterbacks. It's also about six percentage points below league average. Let's add some context.

When Cousins was pressured, it took 2.59 seconds, the 12th-highest mark. Cousins only threw the ball deep twice. Fifty-nine percent of his throws were short, and he averaged -1.75 air yards to the sticks. So, it's not as if the pass rush had ample time to get after the Raiders quarterback.

You'd still like to see at least one sack or more pressure, given the game script and knowing Cousins was going to drop back as often as he did.

Odighizuwa had three pressures. DeForest Buckner had a couple of pressures. James Thompson and Garcen Halton had one. Nick Bosa had two, while Romello Height and Keion White had one. However, only Odgizhuwa had a double-digit pressure rate.

It was Bosa's first game back since September 20. Next week's opponent and their passing attack will be a better gauge of the defensive line and pass rush.

Cousins threw three interceptions, had only 216 passing yards, and posted a QBR of just 60.0. He didn't play well.

Trent Williams didn't get beaten on the sack he allowed. It was a strange play. Williams and Connor Colby allowed a pair of pressures, while Colton McKivitz and Jake Brendel allowed one. The offensive line combined for 17 impact plays.

Purdy was blitzed 43.3% of the time but was sacked only once and pressured 33.3% of the time.

Kaelon Black averaged 1.64 yards before contact on 11 attempts. Christian McCaffrey averaged 1.0 yards before contact on his one carry, and Jordan James averaged 1.67 yards before contact on his one carry. Four carries of 10+ yards, and only three of the 43 carries were stuffed.

The 49ers offensive line continues to excel.

Brown allowed a big play to Bowers, but he had two critical tackles in this game. The one nobody is talking about happened on the 54-yard kickoff return by the Raiders. That saved a touchdown. Las Vegas ended up kicking a 50+ yard field goal after that tackle. On Brown's other tackle at the goal line, the Raiders didn't score.

Malik, too, allowed an explosive pass. All three safeties did. Mustapha made up for it with an interception and a pass breakup. He didn't have to do much in the run game, but Mustapha played well in his deep quarter or third. He'll be the X-factor on this side of the ball moving forward.

I try to avoid listing the best players weekly. Take Trent Williams, for example. He held Maxx Crosby—arguably the best pass rusher in the game—to a 7.4% pressure rate. We heard from Crosby once all game.

George Kittle should be on the winners list every week. He's assumed Mike Evans' role, judging by the workload. Purdy targeted Kittle on 37.5 of the late downs and another 25% in the red zone. Kittle had four receptions for 58 yards and set the tone early with a 37-yarder. He was arguably the 49ers' most impactful player in Week 9.

The broadcast said Warner had a quiet game, but he had a tackle for loss and a forced fumble when the Raiders were trailing by 11 and a first down away from the red zone. That was the game-changing play the defense needed.

#2756. Caleb Williams, Baker Mayfield turn 'SNF' into turnover fest as Bears overcome interceptions

Caleb Williams, Baker Mayfield turn 'SNF' into turnover fest as Bears overcome interceptions originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Through a combined 10 games this season, Baker Mayfield and Caleb Williams totaled just three interceptions.

For Williams, his limited turnovers have helped lead an elite Bears offense, emerging as a potential Super Bowl contender. Mayfield has also historically taken care of the football; despite playing through injuries in 2026, the veteran has at least been careful with his passes.

But both former Heisman Trophy winners and No. 1 overall picks had an ugly showing on "Sunday Night Football," which wound up being a game full of turnovers. Mayfield and Williams combined for six interceptions — and it led to a wacky, back-and-forth game between the Bears and Buccaneers, which Chicago narrowly escaped with a 27-24 win.

Here's a look at the collection of interceptions between Williams and Mayfield on Sunday night and how the Bears still overcame their turnovers.

MORE: Which NFL trade candidates boosted - or hurt - their stocks in Week 9?

Williams has always been a fairly accurate quarterback who rarely turns the ball over — he came into 2026 with 13 interceptions in two full seasons. Through five games, he was playing a clean brand of football, only throwing a pick in September against the Vikings.

Everything unraveled for Williams against Tampa Bay, though. With the Bears pushed back beyond the 20-yard line on a 2nd & Goal, Williams went for a shot to the end zone that wound up hurting Chicago in the second quarter.

Tykee Smith grabbed the first Williams pick of the night.

Just minutes later, after a Buccaneers turnover on downs, Williams' next throw was also an interception to Zyon McCollum.

The end of the second quarter wound up being littered with turnovers. Right after getting the ball back with a chance to add points before halftime, McCollum got him again, diving to make an interception that was awarded after a review.

The fourth Williams interception was less his fault than just an unfortunate bounce. Looking for Luther Burden in the third quarter, the pass bounced off Burden into the air, and Alex Anzalone was in the perfect spot to make the play.

It became the first four-interception game of Williams' NFL career.

MORE:4 quarterbacks who boosted their early MVP cases in Week 9

Mayfield, meanwhile, had not thrown multiple interceptions in a game since last December against the Dolphins. After not throwing a single pass against the Falcons last week, he put the ball in a bit more danger in Week 9.

Toward the end of the first half, Mayfield was looking for Tez Johnson when Cam Lewis picked him off.

The far more critical interception came in the third quarter. Mayfield, moments after Williams' fourth pick, was trying to get the ball to Cade Otton for a short gain when Jaylon Johnson jumped in front of the tight end, grabbed the ball, then got a block en route to the end zone.

The pick-six made it a two-score Bears lead, and it was the sixth interception of the game between the two teams.

For a moment, it appeared the Bears' fifth turnover of the night would be the final nail in the coffin, despite their late lead.

Reuben Bain had a huge hit on Luther Burden with under four minutes remaining that knocked the ball free, also sending the wide receiver to the locker room with an injury. The fumble was recovered by Tampa Bay, which trailed by eight.

Mayfield's clutch gene showed up in the big moment. Bucky Irving powered into the end zone for a touchdown, then Mayfield ran one in for the two-point conversion that tied it at 24 as Burden's fumble cost the Bears.

In the end, though, Williams and the Bears' offense showed up when it mattered most. Chicago drove 49 yards over eight plays, highlighted by a 38-yard pass to Colston Loveland, setting up a game-winning field goal from Cairo Santos.

Despite five turnovers, nine penalties and a blown late lead, Chicago found a way to improve to 7-2, looking more like a Super Bowl contender with every game that passes.

#2752. 5 momentous plays in Week 9: Commanders fail late again, and Browns' gutsy OT call

Sunday's early action didn't exactly stir up some hot Monday debate. Several games were blowouts, with only five decided by one score.

There were some fascinating close-game finishes, however, and a few non-one-score games whose fates could have looked far different had one or two plays gone the other way.

The Washington Commanders, frequent guests of this list, have ended up here again thanks to a few fateful late plays in their failed upset bid against the Los Angeles Rams.

The Browns lost their second straight, and it came down to a critical call by head coach Todd Monken late in overtime.

We also had one of the wilder interceptions you'll ever see … and to think that it almost didn't go down as a pick added to the drama.

Here are five of the more momentous plays from Sunday's early action:

The Commanders had their opportunities to beat the Rams, but a mix of stalled drives and a messy final play doomed them in the end.

After the Rams took a 20-13 lead early in the fourth on Puka Nacua's touchdown, Washington got the ball back multiple times but couldn't generate points. Two drives in the final five-plus minutes ended without scoring.

The botched final play was the talking point after the game went final. Jayden Daniels threw short of the end zone, with the Commanders at the Rams' 32-yard line, and the ball was lateraled several times before being recovered by the Rams.

Let's be clear: It wasn't a well-thought-out plan. The fact Daniels didn't throw either of his final two passes into the end zone, with no timeouts, is damning.

But the sequence that really turned the game came near the two-minute warning. With 2:31 remaining at the Rams' 38-yard line, Daniels took a 12-yard sack by Myles Garrett, one of three on the day for him. Washington head coach Dan Quinn opted to let the clock run down to the two-minute stoppage.

On fourth-and-17, Daniels' deep throw to Van Jefferson was broken up by the Rams' Emmanuel Forbes Jr. at the last second.

Forbes, a former Commander, was guilty of a bad penalty and was beaten for a touchdown by Jefferson earlier in the game. Washington had a target on him, but Forbes made the big play.

The Browns were a 2-point conversion away from a 6-3 record. The Saints had taken a seven-point lead in overtime Sunday at the Superdome, but Shedeur Sanders — starting for an injured Deshaun Watson — rallied the Browns with a touchdown pass of his own with one minute left in OT.

Then came the decision: kick or go for it?

Browns head coach Todd Monken opted to go for 2 and the win. The play was doomed from the start. Sanders' hurried pass fell incomplete, and for good measure, the Browns' Tytus Howard was flagged for holding.

If you want to argue the play-call, be my guest. It looked like Sanders tried to make something happen out of structure, but it just wasn't there.

But Monken's call? I was fine with it. It felt like an aggressive, but highly defensible decision given where the Browns were at. They failed to get in on a fourth-and-goal situation earlier in the game and turned it over again on downs early in the fourth quarter. The Saints were at home, and the Browns were fortunate even to be in overtime after the Saints took the lead with less than a minute remaining.

Had they opted to tie it, the Saints would have had a full minute left and two timeouts. Tyler Shough led a six-play, 55-yard TD drive that took only 50 seconds earlier in the game.

Monken went for the win, and it's hard to blame him for that.

Nik Bonitto's strip sack on the Panthers' final drive sealed Sunday's Broncos win over the Panthers. But Devon Key's end-zone interception was also a massive play — and the kind you have to see to believe.

Late in the third quarter, Carolina led, 14-13, and had first-and-goal at the Denver 1. Bryce Young threw to a diving Tommy Tremble in the left corner of the end zone, with the ball hitting Tremble's hands twice before being ruled incomplete.

But wait! Replay saw what many others watching on television likely did: Key somehow catching the ball before it touched the ground.

Key smiled after the play, apparently not knowing he actually caught the ball. The fact that he had to whip his body almost completely around to make the play also added a serious degree of difficulty to the act.

What's wild is that Carolina had actually snapped the ball for second-and-goal, with Chuba Hubbard stopped for no gain, but Broncos head coach Sean Payton's challenge flag had come out prior to that snap.

There was also more controversy with the play, as Panthers fans wanted Denver's Justin Strnad flagged for pass interference, but there was no call.

The pick prevented at least three points, and possibly seven. So instead of a 4-to-8 point lead for the Panthers, they were only up one — briefly. The Broncos went down and scored a touchdown on their ensuing drive. After missing the 2-point try, Denver then led, 19-14.

It was a massive momentum swing in the game and a big reason why the Broncos held on to win.

The Falcons were their own worst enemies in their 34-17 loss to the Bengals in Spain. Penalties were one of the biggest problems for Atlanta — especially one critical flag.

Early in the fourth quarter, the Falcons trailed, 31-17, but they had the ball at the Cincinnati 10-yard line, facing a third-and-2. Kyle Pitts Sr. was called for holding, pushing the ball back to the 20 and making it fourth-and-12. The pass was incomplete on the hold, so it didn't wipe anything out, but their decision would have been a lot different on fourth-and-2.

Falcons head coach Kevin Stefanski opted for the field goal with 11:27 left … but Nick Folk pushed a 38-yarder wide left. Atlanta marched 11 plays and 52 yards and got nothing out of it.

The Falcons were flagged 12 times for 88 yards in the game. Earlier in the contest, they reached the Bengals' 7-yard line before multiple offensive line flags pushed them back and compelled them to settle for a field goal.

Atlanta had other things it needed to clean up in the Spain game, but the penalties really cost the Falcons. They never seriously threatened again after Folk's miss.

The Giants continued to win ugly Sunday, and they needed help from their benched quarterback to get it done. Jameis Winston lost his starting job to J.J. McCarthy, but when McCarthy got hurt against the Eagles, in came Winston for a crucial, go-ahead touchdown drive in the third quarter.

To that point, the Giants had kicked only field goals and trailed, 8-6. Early in the third quarter, McCarthy took a hit from the Eagles' Zack Baun and left with a hip injury. Winston took over and completed 6-of-8 passes for 71 yards, including a 5-yard TD throw to Isaiah Likely. Winston also converted a fourth-and-1 with a sneak.

That put the Giants up, 13-8. The Eagles never got the lead back. McCarthy eventually made it back in, and the Giants hung on for a 19-11 win. There's no controversy over who's starting Thursday against the Commanders, but the Giants might not have won this one without Winston's terrific drive.

#2751. Raiders return to Bay Area — and the jail at Levi's Stadium could be busy

The Raiders left Oakland, for the second time, after the 2019 season. On Sunday, the Raiders return to the Bay Area for their first regular-season game since relocating to Las Vegas.

And, as Matt Barrows of The Athletic notes, the renewal of the rivalry could result in plenty of activity in the 3,500-square-foot holding facility at Levi’s Stadium.

The Raiders played a regular-season game there in 2018. That day, there were 32 arrests. It was the first time the 49ers had hosted a Raiders game since a preseason contest in 2011 that featured multiple incidents of fan violence resulted in a suspension of the annual low-travel-cost exhibition get-together.

Regardless of whether things get interesting in the stands, concourses, parking lots, etc., the game brings plenty of on-field intrigue. Raiders quarterback Kirk Cousins and 49ers coach Kyle Shanahan have a relationship that dates back to Cousins’s rookie year with Washington, in 2012. 49ers defensive coordinator Raheem Morris was Atlanta’s head coach during Cousins’s two seasons with the Falcons.

And Raiders coach Klint Kubiak was the 49ers’ passing game coordinator in 2023. Klint’s brother, Klay, has been the 49ers’ offensive coordinator since 2025.

The Raiders are unexpectedly in contention for an AFC playoff berth, at 4-4. The 49ers control the NFC West at 6-1, and they’re in position to force the road to the Super Bowl through Santa Clara.

#2747. 49ers return to winning ways coming off bye week: Winners, losers vs Raiders

The San Francisco 49ers were fresh off their bye week and it showed with a 38-17 Week 9 win, a completely dominant outing against the Las Vegas Raiders on Sunday, Nov. 8 at Levi's Stadium in Santa Clara, California.

The Niners improved to 7-1 behind a stellar defensive performance that forced the Raiders into five turnovers and held their run game to 24 total yards.

Offensively, the 49ers looked like they picked up where they left off. Despite playing without wideouts Mike Evans, Deebo Samuel Sr. and Christian Kirk, they found a way to keep their offense explosive and rolling.

Quarterback Brock Purdy completed 17-of-25 passes and threw for 232 yards and two touchdowns, eclipsing his 100th career passing touchdown. He also ran for 40 yards and touchdown, as his game continues to give MVP considerations.

"It's been amazing. Just since the first game he got in there," Niners head coach Kyle Shanahan said. "Brock's been the man since he's come in and that's a pretty unbelievable deal considering where he was drafted, how he started. Very fortunate to have Brock."

32 THINGS WE LEARNED: QB crises testing teams as backups flounder

Running back Christian McCaffrey tied Hall of Famer Joe Perry for most consecutive games with a rushing touchdown. It was McCaffrey's seventh straight score. He had 18 carries for 68 yards. He added two catches for 19 yards and a touchdown.

The game featured a historic rivalry between two franchises that were once separated by the Bay Bridge. When the Raiders were located in Oakland, it made for the one of the best regional rivalries in sports. Although it's not the same since the Raiders moved to Vegas, it will always be revered by the fans as such.

With Sunday's win, the 49ers took a 9-7 all-time advantage against the Raiders. Here are winners and losers from the matchup:

The 49ers defense forced three turnovers in 3rd quarter, alone. San Francisco forced Las Vegas into an interception by rookie Jaden Dugger who took it back 35 yards for a touchdown on the first drive of the second half. Their two forced fumbles were caused by Fred Warner and Upton Stout.

"That was honestly the emphasis going into this week," Warner said. "I think we just had a spurt where there was a drought in takeaways, and we know how important that is to winning football games and for our team moving forward. ... to come out in the second half and have five of those was unbelievable. It was honestly just guys being in the right spot, doing their job, playing relentlessly, getting to the football and having it on their mind. So, it was definitely a point of emphasis."

Defensive edge Nick Bosa returned from a five-game absence nursing back from knee and calf injuries. DeForest Buckner returned to the 49ers after being traded by the Indianapolis Colts. Each had a tackle, but it's their presence that has things feeling nostalgic for San Francisco.

"Those are two super disruptive players for our D-line, and the offense has to have a plan for them at all times," Warner said. "It frees up a lot of things for the rest of the defense. Of course, I've got to go back and watch the film to really see just how big of an impact they had. But, I mean, you can't not be happy with the fact that we have both of them out on the field for us going forward."

Buckner said he's home again. He was the last name called during their starting lineup introductions. Warner told him to go last.

"It felt great, you know what I mean? Being able to go out there with those guys and fly around on defense, I mean, you could just feel the energy," Buckner said. "You got to fly around to the ball to go make plays, and that's the fun part about it. Not only competing with the Raiders, but with your fellow teammates, because everybody's hungry to make plays."

This just felt necessary to point out that Warner's signature punch out caused so many fumbles. Warner had one, and the fumble that Stout caused to save a touchdown was exactly how Warner would have done it himself.

Jordan Watkins finally had the breakout game that he's capable of. Watkins put up four catches for 86 yards and a touchdown.

"A huge step forward," Shanahan said. "He hasn't gotten his opportunities and some guys have been down andhe's been working and he's been ready for it. I think he's gotten better each week. I think the first week he came out there and played good right away. The ball went his way more this game and he was ready for those opportunities. I thought he's blocked well and stuff. One penalty he'd like to take back, but we can forgive him. That third down was huge. Just the man coverage and going up and making that play on the post. That was a hell of a play."

The 49ers suffered a blow to their defensive line as rookie Gracen Halton broke his ankle and is expected to miss the rest of the season. Their starting cornerbacks Renardo Green and Deommodore Lenoir went out with injuries, but Shanahan doesn't expect them to miss the next week.

The Las Vegas Raiders turned the ball over five times, including three times in the third quarter. The Raiders had three costly turnovers in the game when they were in prime scoring position. Quarterback Kirk Cousins threw three interceptions in the loss, one returned for a touchdown. The Raiders lost an additional two fumbles.

You can credit the 49ers defense if you want, but the Raiders' run game was nonexistent. Ashton Jeanty, the former No. 6 overall pick in the 2025 NFL Draft, had just 25 yards on six touches.

Mike Washington Jr. was supposed to see more touches this week. He finished with -3 yards on six carries.

In Northern California, the Raiders-49ers rivalry is very much alive and fans throughout the region talked trash in the days leading up. Ok, let's be real, as soon as the game was scheduled, the chatter began. There was much anticipation for the game only for 49ers fans to stick it to Raider fans, again. The last time the teams faced in a regular-season was 2023 when the 49ers won 37–34 in overtime on Jan. 1, 2023 in Week 17 of the 2022 NFL season. It's all love between both fanbases. Still, the 49ers get the last laugh.

#2746. Pröschtli! Swiss Devils Lead the Way for Connor Hellebuyck’s 4-0 Shutout of Maple Leafs

For weeks, we've been watching as several Devils have been doing the little things that help win hockey games. Connor Brown has become a mongoose around the net, mixing it up with energy in close and being rewarded with goals. Cody Glass has been forechecking like a machine, forcing opposing defenders to move faster and causing turnovers that lead to Devils chances. Even Jack Hughes, the Devils' most dangerous scoring threat, has been backchecking like his life depends on it. All over the ice, the Devils are doing things that don't necessarily end up on the scoresheet, but are some of the most critical things that contribute to wins.

Since the beginning of the season, the poster child for this kind of hockey has been Timo Meier. Timo has been using his big frame to cause havoc in front of opposing goalies, gotten his stick in passing lanes above the dots in the defensive zone, and taking the body on forwards in the neutral zone. That said, Timo has caught some flack this season for not getting on the scoresheet like some believe he should. He's been firing away, but despite playing some damn good hockey, just hasn't lit the lamp yet. Tonight, that changed.

Timo busted out tonight, potting two goals to go along with goals by Nico and the utility knife, Connor Brown. If he can use this game as a launching pad to start racking up goals while still playing a complete game like he has been, he will be a real… Swiss army knife.

The game started relatively slowly with neither team registering a shot on goal through the first four minutes of play. Even almost 14 minutes into the game, each team had only taken three shots on the opposing netminder. For their part, the Devils' shots to this point had largely been kept to outside the faceoff dots, with Anthony Stolarz handling the long-range pucks with relative ease. Same in the Devils' end, where most shots came from the point, and Hellebuyck kicked out most rubber with little drama.

Aside from a few weird bounces that led to shots in close at funky angles, everything was pretty even-keel throughout the opening period. Just a quiet beginning to the game.

The Devils outshot the Leafs 7-4 in the first, but both teams go scoreless. We go into the locker room knotted at 0.

The second period began much faster than the first, with the Devils looking particularly energetic. I mention it in a little more detail below, but Jesper Bratt had a strong move to the net that started from the right point. When a player can start his drive to the net from where some players tee-up point shots, you know he has a strong stride. In any event, Bratt's move inside was saved.

Off an offensive zone draw to Stolarz's right, Nico wins the puck back to Declan Chisholm who sends a dart across toward the center of the ice. While the scrum at the dot was going on (including the Leafs' McCabe and Nylander running into one another), Timo Meier quietly drifted toward the middle of the ice, between the hash marks. Chisholm's dart made its way through some players to Meier who took one of the most "this-puck-better-go-into-the-back-of-the-frigging-net" follow-throughs I've ever seen. Timo absolutely unloaded on Chisholm's pass, and it did indeed find the back of the net. Timo's heating up! 1-0, Devils!

After the goal, the Devils started firing from everywhere, trying to test Stolarz. Timo started feeling it, shooting from outside the dot. Evan Rodrigues took a shot from just about on the boards. Just rubber flying from everywhere. I really liked seeing this; old Devils teams would sit back and grip their sticks a bit harder after getting a lead. This team seems to smell blood in the water when they get a lead.

This approach paid off at the 12:44 mark, when Jack Hughes took a drop pass from Jesper Bratt between the circles and made a quick move to the center of the ice where he took a quick, heavy shot on goal. Timo Meier, he of the big body, was camped in front of the net and managed to get a stick on Jack's shot, deflecting it upward and into the net. After having only scored one goal to this point in the season, Timo is on the scoresheet with two! Devils lead, 2-0!

Although I've been talking up Timo in this recap, the latter minutes of the second period really went to Connor Hellebuyck, who did well to keep rebounds wide and kick the puck out when there was any danger. He only faced a handful of shots through two periods, but none of that handful felt truly dangerous. To this point in the game, Connor and Timo have the Devils looking firmly in control. Everyone goes into the intermission to get orange slices with the Devils up 2-0.

Early in the third period, Jack Hughes snuck in behind the Toronto defense to collect a bouncing home-run pass and get a breakaway on Stolarz, who made an excellent glove save on Jack's shot.

A few minutes later, Jack shows how much his game has grown in the last year. Of course, we expect him to get those breakaways and give fits to opposing goalies. Tonight, just after the five-minute mark in the third period while the Devils were on a power play, the Leafs had the opportunity to break up ice for a shorthanded try. Except there was one Devil back to responsibly cover for his defenders… Jack Hughes. I absolutely love seeing this from Jack. Although Toronto got the shot off, it was easily handled by Hellebuyck. Just a huge, responsible play by someone we're seeing mature into a complete player.

Around the eight-minute mark, Hellebuyck makes one of his best saves of the night, robbing Auston Matthews with a quick glove. If Matthews scores there, it changes the entire complexion of the game. Instead, just over a minute later, Nico Hischier buries a rebound on a post-toe-drag shot by Jack Hughes to put the game out of reach. The Swiss-trick makes it 3-0, Devils!

To close out the game, Cody Glass absolutely outhustled Gavin McKenna to get to a loose puck along the boards, which he then dished to Connor Brown at the center of the ice. Brown made no mistake from long distance, burying the dagger for the final goal of the game. Brown will be on the scoresheet, but that goal was all Cody Glass and his hustle. Regardless, 4-0, Devils! Game!

Another two points for the Devils, and Connor Hellebuyck gets his first shutout in a Devils sweater.Helle yes! Let's keep it rolling!

In my preview for this game, I wrote that one of the things that Connor Hellebuyck seems to bring to the Devils is a measure of confidence. Since he arrived in New Jersey, it looks to me like the Devils play with a bit more enthusiasm than they do in front of Jake Allen. This is not to say that Jake Allen is a bad goalie. As I noted in my preview, he's served admirably in New Jersey. It's just that Hellebuyck's goaltending style seems so calm and measured, and that poise rubs off on his teammates in front of him.

Having played goal, I know how hard it can be to find the balance between cutting the angle on a shooter and being cognizant of players on your periphery that could score easily on a rebound. Goalies are in a constant state of trying to reduce the amount of net a shooter can see while also accounting for other potential threats. Hellebuyck's movements in net are minimized because has mastered this balance. I can't think of another goalie in recent memory that effectively plays the geometry of the position as well as he does.

This is where I think his effectiveness, and in turn his teammates' confidence, comes from. He plays his angles perfectly and has fantastic rebound control. Because he doesn't need to worry about easy rebounds falling to opponents, he can be more aggressive at the top of his crease and take away huge parts of the net for his opponents to shoot at. Because of that, he doesn't need to move much to make saves.

Someone in the comments said that Hellebuyck makes difficult saves look easy by barely moving when he makes them. That's the mark of a great goaltender. We already knew he was special, but now that I see him up close in the red and black, I think he has taken the Devils to another level.

I don't want to spend too much time stating the obvious, but Jesper Bratt's rush to the outside with his power move to the net during the second period showed the kind of jets he has. Jack gets most of the praise for his skill and speed, but I firmly believe that Bratt is nearly at his level.

Much like Timo, Bratt has caught some criticism about not scoring like he should (and passing much more often than he should), but even during a "slow" start to the season, his speed and stick-handling have him toward the top of the Devils roster in terms of points. It seems like only a matter of time before he breaks out as well.

And if the Devils can get simultaneous and consistent scoring from Jack, Nico, Timo, and Bratt… I'd take them against any team in the league.

Nothing profound here. I'm just really enjoying a sustained stretch of Devils hockey for the first time in what seems like a long time. It rules.

This is an inclusion from Chris: I really love these old-school style photos. Credit to Mark Blinch for a great photograph of Timo Meier and Oliver Ekman-Larsson. There were a few more shot in this style during the game, but this is probably the best action photo that Blinch got with it.

Connor Hellebuyck returns to Winnipeg as the Devils take on the Jets on Tuesday at 7:00PM

#2745. Video emerges of parking lot brawl at Raiders-49ers game

It remains to be seen whether the first Raiders game at Levi’s Stadium in eight years matched or exceeded the 32 arrests made in the building in 2018.

In the parking lot, there was some mayhem.

Via Grant Young of the New York Post, video has surfaced of a brawl involving several people. Multiple individuals seemed to be rendered unconscious.

Fan violence resulted in Raiders and 49ers suspending their annual postseason game after the 2011 season. They play in the regular season at least once every four years, with the Raiders visiting the 49ers at least once every eight years.

It’s impossible to eliminate all fighting. When it happens, the goal becomes finding the folks who violated the law and holding them accountable. Deterrence is the only way to minimize such issues.

For folks who value their safety and well-being, scenes like the one that played out on Sunday are all the more reason to watch the games at home.

#2744. Kings send Nique Clifford to Thunder for draft assets: Trade grades

The Sacramento Kings have agreed to trade guard Nique Clifford to the Oklahoma City Thunder in exchange for an unprotected 2028 second-round draft pick via Utah and an unprotected 2030 second-round draft pick via Minnesota, the team announced Sunday, Nov. 8.

Clifford, 24, is a 6-foot-5, 210-pound guard out of Colorado State that was originally selected by Oklahoma City with the 24th overall pick in the first round of the 2025 NBA Draft. However, Clifford never logged a minute in a Thunder uniform following a trade to the Kings that sent Oklahoma City a protected 2027 first-round pick.

He appeared in 75 games for Sacramento during the 2025-26 season and started 28. Clifford averaged 8.6 points, 3.8 rebounds and 2.4 assists in about 25.1 minutes during his rookie campaign with the Kings, while shooting 41.8% from the field and 33% on 3-pointers.

Clifford was being molded into a perennial "three-and-D" type of player for the Kings. He saw a lot of playing time in his rookie campaign, in part due to the numerous injuries Sacramento sustained to its roster that year.

He scored a career-high 30 points in a 132-126 loss to the Cleveland Cavaliers on Feb. 8. Throughout the 2025-26 season, Clifford also spent some time down in the G-League, playing for the Stockton Kings, the Kings affiliate.

In his collegiate years, Clifford averaged 10.1 points and just under a steal. He shot 46.9% from the field and 36.4% on 3-pointers.

Clifford signed a four-year rookie contract with Kings worth up to $15.7 million with $6.3 million guaranteed, according to Spotrac. Clifford's average annual salary is about $3.9 million.

He'll earn another $3.2 million in base salary.

Clifford appeared to be a contributing piece to the Kings' rebuild. The consummate professional, Clifford came into work, did what he was asked and pushed himself to be the best version of himself.

There was an expectation that Clifford would take a leap into his second-year but instead it came with setbacks. Clifford saw his minutes diminish early in the season. In his last game against the San Antonio Spurs on Friday, Nov. 6, Clifford only logged 4 minutes, 30 seconds and scored five points on 2-of-2 from the field.

He only played more than 10 minutes once this season – against the Memphis Grizzlies on Oct. 31. Clifford appeared in five of their nine games, earning four DNPs by coach's decision.

It appeared that Clifford was on the opposite end of head coach Doug Christie's rotations and a change was warranted. The Kings get to open a roster spot while picking up assets.

The Thunder have had misfortune early in the season, facing season-ending injuries to key rotation players, and additional injuries that will keep guys out long-term. Jalen Williams suffered a fractured left fibula and a dislocated ankle that required surgery. He's out for the year.

Cason Wallace has a torn labrum in his left shoulder and he is also out indefinitely. Chet Holmgren was ruled out 8 to 10 weeks after a biological treatment for a small patellar tendon tear in his right knee.

Oklahoma City needs bodies. Clifford was being buried on the bench early this season, the Thunder believe there's a spark that he can bring to to the team.

The organization has built a reputation of maximizing reserves and role players getting the best out of them, the latest project will be Clifford.

#2743. Texans blow out Chargers 44-22 as C.J. Stroud throws five TDs

The Texans might not be back, considering it was the Chargers they blew out, but they can’t be counted out just yet either.

Ladd McConkey had back spasms in pregame warmups and didn’t play.

#2741. Hornets' Kon Knueppel out for 4 weeks with left hamstring strain, Dennis Schröder to also miss time

The Charlotte Hornets have confirmed that forward Kon Knueppel and veteran guard Dennis Schröder will both miss time. Both players are out after suffering the same injury, a left hamstring strain, but their return timelines are different: Knueppel is set to be re-evaluated in four weeks, while Schröder will be re-evaluated in two.

Knueppel's injury appears to be a reaggravation of a left hamstring strain that sidelined him for the start of the season. The initial injury occurred during an "offseason on-court activity," per the team.

Knueppel finally made his season debut on Wednesday against the Brooklyn Nets, but left the game in the first half after pulling up awkwardly.

Neither Knueppel nor Schröder played in Friday's NBA Cup matchup against the Boston Celtics; that game ended in a 119-80 rout.

Knueppel, who finished second in Rookie of the Year voting last season after a thrilling race with eventual winner Cooper Flagg, was set to be a key piece of the Hornets' offense this season. With him sidelined, the Hornets have started the year with a disappointing 3-7 run.

Schröder was sent to Charlotte before the season — his ninth time being traded in his career. Across the first nine games of the year, Schröder established himself as the team's fourth-highest scorer and currently leads the team in assists.
